#190. GTA 6 sales hit $3.8bn in a single day - the biggest entertainment launch in history

Grand Theft Auto 6 (GTA 6) saw record retail sales in its first full-day of release, hitting $3.8bn (Â£2.9bn), making it the biggest entertainment launch in history.

Take-Two, the parent company of Rockstar Games, said on Friday the figures were based on estimates through end of day on Thursday and marked a record for "entertainment across any medium". It pre-sold 32.5m copies of the game.

To compare, Spider-Man: Brand New Day, this year's highest-grossing film so far, made $2.5bn (Â£1.8bn) over its entire run in cinemas.

Internet infrastructure firm Cloudflare said earlier that PS5 downloads in the UK peaked at 8.6 times normal levels around its launch.

New Zealand, where GTA 6 was first available, saw a 14.8-fold spike four hours before, suggesting some players switched regions for early access.

In its initial findings since the game's launch, Cloudflare also recorded a huge surge in traffic to Rockstar's online services, peaking at 11.2 times normal levels in Australia and 9.3 times on the US East Coast.

In Europe, UK interest was particularly strong, reaching seven times normal levels compared with 5.3 times in France and Germany.

The record smashes that of its predecessor - from 13 years prior - Grand Theft Auto V. The game previously held the best opening week sales in video-game history, earning $1.15bn (Â£1.2bn) in just five days.

The game is the latest instalment in Rockstar's nearly three decade old crime simulator franchise, which has become one of the most popular games in history.

At its core, the series is a satirical take on the American dream, putting players in the shoes of career criminals in Leonida, the game's Florida-inspired state.

The latest release follows new characters, a pair of small-time criminals Lucia and her partner Jason.

GTA 6 is estimated to have cost more than $1bn to develop, which would make it the most expensive video game in history.

BBC News Mundoâs Rafael Rojas visits the real-life Miami locations that inspired GTA 6's Vice City.

Research firm Sensor Tower said GTA 6 will be so popular that it will reduce the time gamers spend on other single-player titles by 10%.

The firm said its estimates are based on email receipts data and other statistical sources that track millions of people daily. They also track public profiles across major gaming platforms like PlayStation and Xbox, where GTA 6 is available.

Around three quarters of the game's sales have been on PlayStation 5 and the balance on Xbox, Sensor Tower said.

Niko Partners, a gaming market research firm, said it expects GTA 6 to make more than $3bn in sales worldwide this year.

Witness History: The launch of Grand Theft Auto

That figure would rank GTA 6 among the highest-grossing entertainment releases ever, but it would still have some way to go to catch 1939's Gone with the Wind, which has made an estimated $4.5bn (Â£3.44bn), adjusted for inflation.

Rockstar is expected to make even more money from GTA 6 over the coming years.

The New York-based studio has yet to announce when the game will be released for PCs or given a date for its multiplayer expansion, which will allow players to interact and go on missions with others over the internet.

If 2013's GTA 5 is any guide, multiplayer will add dozens more hours of gameplay and bring in even more revenue for Rockstar as players spend real money on in-game property, cars and other perks.

GTA 6 launched around the world on 19 November

Anticipation for the blockbuster title has grown through repeated delays, following a 13-year wait.

GTA 6 has been hailed by some reviewers as one of the most impressive gaming experiences ever. Entertainment media site IGN described it as an "uncompromisingly engineered" masterpiece.

The release of GTA 6 at midnight on Thursday across various time zones saw many gamers stay up through the night, while some took the following day off to play.

The launch broke Virgin Media O2's evening traffic record as people rushed to install GTA 6's huge 212GB file on consoles.

The game's graphic content has resulted in censorship in Japan and, like earlier GTA releases, is not approved for sale in mainland China.

Police departments have also reminded Americans that street racing, auto theft and gun violence are still crimes in real life, as excitement reached a fever pitch in the US.

#186. Tech stock sell-off drags markets down as Anthropic shares slide

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off dragged markets down.

The sell-off comes as concerns remain over the speed and size of artificial intelligence investment, but also as some large companies including Nvidia and SpaceX faced specific issues that worried investors.

Those pressures combined to weigh on US markets through Friday, with all key US indexes closing sharply down on the day, furthering earlier falls over the week.

Both the S&P 500 and the Dow Jones Industrial Average were pushed into correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The S&P 500 closed down 3.7%, the Dow Jones Industrial Average was down 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.3%. However, all three indexes were up on where they were a year ago.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Investors were also spooked after the Democrats' resounding win in the US midterms in early November, which sparked fears of a reduction in AI development.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping at one point by more than 12% to about $85, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83. It closed down 8.5%, reversing half of the gains made on Thursday.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50 before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday as the market sell-off continued.

Nvidia shares had plummeted by 13.7% by the close of trade, following Reuters reports - unconfirmed by the BBC - the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission over its accounting practices.

Musk's firms Tesla and SpaceX both experienced falls of more than 7.6%. SpaceX shares were hit after it announced a bond sale to finance what the company said was the largest purchase of Nvidia chips on record.

Meta closed down more than 5% and Amazon was down more than 4%, while Apple and Alphabet experienced smaller falls.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

#183. Average 401(k), IRA balances fall as more workers tap their accounts, Fidelity says

Retirement account balances fell slightly in the third quarter amid market volatility, according to data released Monday from Fidelity Investments, the nation's largest provider of 401(k) savings plans.

Some workers also tapped their retirement accounts for cash, signaling that persistent affordability pressures continue to strain household finances, said Mike Shamrell, Fidelity's vice president of thought leadership.

The average 401(k) balance slipped 2% from the previous quarter to $152,500, while the average individual retirement account balance was down 1% to $142,261, Fidelity found. Both figures hit record highs during the second quarter.

Despite the third-quarter decline, average balances in IRAs, 401(k)s and 403(b) plans — which are 401(k)-type plans offered by entities such as public schools, churches and nonprofits — are higher year over year and continue to trend up over the long run.

Wall Street's major averages finished the third quarter on mixed footing, driven in part by the war with Iran, which blocked passage through the Strait of Hormuz, a key transit artery for global energy supplies, and caused oil prices to spike. The stock market's slowdown in the third quarter marked a cooling after the double-digit gains posted in the previous quarter.

The S&P 500 index added 2.03% over the three-month period, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average fell 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite ended the quarter 2.2% higher.

At the same time, financial pressures pushed more savers to reduce their contributions to retirement plans.

The average 401(k) contribution rate, including employee and employer contributions, edged down to 14.3% from 14.4% in the second quarter, Fidelity found. Fidelity's recommended combined annual savings benchmark is 15%.

"We know that there is a lot of concern out there ... [and] people are likely making changes to their personal financial world," Shamrell said.

Some savers also dipped into their accounts to free up cash, a sign of more acute financial strain amid persistently high prices and ongoing inflationary pressures.

The share of workers with an outstanding loan in the third quarter was 19.6%, up slightly from 19.5% in the previous quarter, according to Fidelity. The percentage of participants taking a new loan from their 401(k) held steady at 2.8%.

Separately, the share of workers taking a hardship withdrawal rose to 2.17% from 2.12% in the second quarter, Fidelity found.

"There is a rise because of economic reasons," said Philip Chao, a certified financial planner and chief investment officer at Experiential Wealth, based in Cabin John, Maryland. "No question about it."

A hardship withdrawal can be taken from a retirement plan without paying an early withdrawal penalty as long as the investor has an "immediate and heavy financial need," according to the IRS, such as avoiding foreclosure, eviction or unforeseen health expenses. Such distributions are also typically taxable.

So-called leakage from 401(k) plans, particularly when workers cash out their accounts before retirement, can significantly erode long-term wealth by undermining the power of compound growth. Withdrawing funds during a market downturn can be especially damaging, making it more difficult to recover losses and benefit from a future rebound, financial advisors say.

However, despite the general consensus that savers shouldn't tap their retirement accounts, there may be situations in which investors don't have another good option, Chao said.

In other words, the financial "ideal" doesn't align with "reality" for households, he said.

Retirement plan withdrawals are happening as rising inflation has pressured households' wages and savings, other data also shows.

"Real" average hourly earnings — or wages after accounting for the rising cost of living — fell 0.7% in the year through September 2026, according to the latest annual figures published by the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Households are saving at their lowest rate since 2022, and consumers are increasingly turning to debt such as credit cards to make ends meet.

Fidelity said earlier this year that 401(k) investors took more loans in 2025 relative to 2024. The findings are consistent with other reports as well.

Vanguard Group, another 401(k) administrator, said in an annual report that savers took a record number of hardship withdrawals last year.

A separate report issued this month by the Plan Sponsor Council of America, a trade group representing employers, found that the share of 401(k) participants with a loan outstanding from their workplace retirement plan rose "significantly" in 2025, nearly doubling from about 12% the prior year.

The percentage of 401(k) investors who took a hardship withdrawal from their plan also increased for the third consecutive year, to 3.4% in 2025 from 1.5% in 2022, according to the PSCA report.

Such "401(k) loans and hardships are there for participants when they need to tap the funds," said Hattie Greenan, the PSCA's research director. "Clearly there's a need right now."

There are situations in which a retirement plan withdrawal could be OK for workers, Chao said.

For example, 401(k) loans are generally a cheaper form of borrowing than credit cards, because investors ultimately pay themselves back with interest, he said.

"If you just need it for a month, two, three, that's fine," Chao said. "It's not going to kill you, borrowing from yourself."

However, a 401(k) loan may make sense only if the saver believes they'll be able to repay it, he said.

Failure to repay a loan can lead to income taxes and penalties that erode long-term savings, Chao said.

Depending on the specific terms of a workplace retirement plan, investors who are laid off or leave their job may have to repay their full loan relatively quickly. Just 34% of plans allow for continued repayment of a loan after separation from service, according to the PSCA report.

#181. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#180. Investigators make progress in hunt for cause of FAA ground stop

Engineers and analysts searching for the root cause of the technical issues behind Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration have been able to replicate the situation that was overwhelming parts of the air traffic system, people familiar with the investigation told CNBC.

While replicating the issue in a lab in Florida is progress, those investigating the matter have yet to determine exactly why two routers in question failed, according to the people, who requested anonymity to talk about an ongoing situation. Finding the root cause is critical to the FAA determining if there may be an issue with other routers that form the backbone of communications for the national air transportation network.

The ground stop lasted for more than four hours Friday, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford have said there is no indication the routers failed due to a cyberattack.

The incident in question happened shortly after L3Harris Technologies, the primary contractor supporting the FAA telecommunications network, installed a new router at a facility in El Dorado, Arkansas.

L3Harris said that it, in partnership with other tech companies, has completed more than 70% of the planned airspace communications conversion from legacy copper wires to a fiber-optic system.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," the statement said. "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

Investigators said within eight minutes of its installation, the router began to transmit too much data within the FAA network.

That surge of data, 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds, destabilized aircraft communications and radar feeds, Duffy said.

Since the ground stop, Duffy has increased the amount of money he wants to upgrade the national air traffic system. After originally asking for $30 billion to build a digital network that will incorporate artificial intelligence, Duffy has now asked Congress for $50 billion.

#178. Stuck: How a frozen housing market is hitting buyers, sellers and the economy

Autumn is usually a busy time in the housing market, but not this year. A sharp spike in mortgage rates in September has pushed the already chilly real estate sector into a deep freeze.

Buyers and sellers are moving to the sidelines, leaving those in the real estate business without much business.

The average rate on the popular 30-year fixed mortgage hovered around 6.60% in July, according to Mortgage News Daily. By the start of October, it was at 7.61%, a full percentage point higher.

"That shock, that rise of 100 basis points, is one of the fastest three-month increases we've seen in decades," said Zillow CEO Jeremy Wacksman on CNBC.

While the jump in rates exacerbated the problem, the slowdown in the housing market has been building for years. Rising home prices have pushed the dream of homeownership out of reach for a growing number of Americans. The number of active buyers nationally has been declining for six straight months and in October hit its lowest level since the aftermath of the financial crisis, according to Redfin.

The supply of homes for sale is also low. Builders are now faced with higher costs for land, labor, materials and energy. They simply can't afford to build the cheaper homes that are currently in demand. Single-family building permits, an indicator of future construction, were down 13% in October year over year, according to the U.S. Census. And builder sentiment in November dropped to the lowest level since 2012.

This slowdown in the entire housing ecosystem is impacting every facet of Americans' lives and livelihoods.

"Where you live determines so much. It determines where your kids go to school. It determines the jobs that you have access to, right? It determines whether or not you're able to be close to your family," said Chen Zhao, chief economist at Redfin. "What we've seen with the housing market these past four years, with this historically low level of turnover, is that a lot of people really are stuck in their home."

Closed sales of existing homes fell to a 16-year low in October, according to the National Association of Realtors. Pending sales, an indicator of future closings, hit a record low on the 25-year-old index from the Realtors.

While the pace of home price increases has slowed recently, prices are up well over 50% from pre-pandemic levels. Part of that is because homebuilding has slowed to a crawl.

It all has Ryan Deighan, who wants to buy a home, wondering if he ever will.

"I genuinely don't know, and that's the scary part," he said.

Deighan and his wife have been renting in Boston for six years. He is a chiropractor; she works in private equity. Even with a combined salary of more than $200,000 a year, he says they're stuck.

"Making what I am and what my wife makes, it just feels like we're on paper doing well, but it feels like we are still kind of fighting to put down certain amounts toward a house," Deighan said.

Being priced out of homeownership, he said, is changing what he called his "checklist" of how he once pictured his future.

"You graduate college, you find someone, you get married, you have the home with the white picket fence and the dog, and then the kids," he said. "That just looks a little different for us."

The numbers don't just affect would-be buyers. They are hitting sellers, too.

Robin Welch has been living in her Cleveland-area home for more than 20 years. Her children are now grown and moved out. She would like to downsize to a condominium, but the math doesn't make sense — in part, because her current interest rate is much lower than any new rate she could get. This has been dubbed the mortgage "lock-in effect."

"It actually costs more money to buy less of a home," said Welch. "I would have to pay into an HOA, which would be another monthly expense on top of increased property taxes and increased interest rate."

Welch has also done upgrades to her home over the years and doesn't want to have to start doing that on another home.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

Welch is part of why the housing turnover rate, which is how often homes get new owners, has now dropped to just 3.8% a year, well below the 5% rate in the decade following the financial crisis, according to Cotality.

In addition, the national mobility rate, which is how often people move from region to region, has been falling for two years. It now sits at 8.3%, down from 11.1% a decade ago, according to Harvard's Joint Center for Housing Studies tabulations of U.S. Census Bureau data. Mobility hit a high of nearly 18% in the late 1980s.

"It's largely because of the interest lock-in effect, but also because of home prices just being so high," said Riordan Frost, senior research analyst at Harvard's JCHS. "Those things combined have made it hard both for existing homeowners to move and for people to get into homeownership."

Americans have nearly $35 trillion of their wealth locked up in housing, a record high, according to Cotality. But the slowdown in the housing market, Frost said, impacts the accumulation, handover and use of that wealth. It also affects the labor market.

"Any kind of potential job would have to really outweigh … the price of taking that mortgage with a higher rate, or the price of moving, or the price of getting into a higher rent unit," Frost said.

The wealth gap between homeowners and renters now is nothing short of massive. The most recent survey of consumer finances last year by the Federal Reserve showed that the median homeowner net wealth is about 43 times the median renter net wealth.

"It has kind of knock-on effects for the rest of the economy. It has effects on the labor market and how good of a match we can get between employees and employers. It has an effect on education. It has an effect on just GDP growth," said Zhao.

When homes aren't selling and people aren't moving, those who work in that business are stuck as well. The entire housing ecosystem is churning much more slowly, and the sudden jump in mortgage rates is causing deals to fall apart.

"Right after Labor Day, we went live with a bunch of listings. They all went under contract over the asking price," said Dana Bull, a real estate agent in the Boston suburbs. But then rates spiked.

"It was a shock to the system," Bull said. "I think all of them came back on the market. We fell out of contract." Asked if she is seeing colleagues leaving the market, she answered: "People are thinking about it. It's tough."

The economy has lost close to 2,000 jobs in real estate and leasing over the past year and 4,400 jobs in residential construction, according to the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Jobs in the mortgage industry have been falling since June and are down 38% since their last peak in the second quarter of 2021, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association. That was when mortgage rates were less than half what they are today.

Shant Banosian, president of Rate and the top mortgage originator in the U.S. by dollar volume, said his business is down about 30% this past year.

"Whenever change happens quickly, there's a slowdown," said Banosian, who has been in the business for 23 years. "It requires more of us to go out and advise, educate and guide. So, what I experienced was I just needed to spend more time on marketing, branding, relationship building. The conversations are longer because people want more information to try to navigate this market."

Bull agrees. She said her job has gotten harder as well.

"We've done double the work this year, having homes go under contract, fall out of contract, putting them back under agreement, but definitely more negotiating," she said. "That's sort of my plea to both buyers and sellers: We need to get back to a space where we're comfortable negotiating."

#177. UK to explore even closer ties with EU after agreeing deal, says Burnham

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen, Prime Minister Andy Burnham and European Council President Antonio Costa join hands at the end of a press conference during the EU-UK summit in Brussels

Andy Burnham said the UK will discuss possible options for even closer ties with the EU, after agreeing a deal covering British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

The prime minister said it was a "good day" for Britain and Europe, adding the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

European Council President AntÃ³nio Costa said the EU remains "open to have the closest possible relationship" with the UK.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal "isn't fair" - and Reform's Nigel Farage called it a "betrayal of the British people".

Legislation to implement the deal is expected to be introduced in the UK Parliament next week.

Burnham arrived in Brussels to finalise the latest stage of the government's post-Brexit "reset" of relations with the EU, which originally began under former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer.

The agreements announced on Friday included scrapping most of the checks on British food exports, in return for the UK following some EU regulations, from 2028.

The UK government believes the changes will cut costs for businesses and could bring prices down for shoppers, while critics argue the change will make the UK a "rule taker" from Brussels rather than a rule maker.

Watch: What the new UK-EU deal could mean for European relations

The new youth experience scheme will allow up to 100,000 18 to 31 year olds from the EU to travel, work and study in the UK for up to three years. The same number of young Britons will be allowed into Europe.

The two sides want to ensure that opportunities under the annual scheme will be available to young people by no later than January 2030.

A further six months is available to discuss a deal on tuition fees, which could prove to be a potential stumbling block.

There have been calls from the European side for fees to be reduced for EU students at British universities. The joint statement says any agreement would also be for UK students in the EU.

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she is "confident" a solution will be found, with Burnham adding: "Me too."

On immigration, the UK and EU have committed to working closely to tackle migrant smuggling and human trafficking. They also expect their law enforcement agencies to share more data.

Burnham said "significant progress" has been made to help "disrupt the gangs who are making those dangerous crossings across the English Channel".

The UK-EU statement also said talks on linking the EU and UK emissions trading systems have concluded and they aim to apply the agreement from 2028.

Watch: Will the UK-EU summit deal boost the economy?

Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

He added: "Beyond that we do need to consider what is the right long-term relationship. Incremental steps only take us so far."

The prime minister said his government will publish a 10-year plan next month, adding it will set out its ambitions for reindustrialisation, growth in "all parts of Britain" and opportunities for young people.

Burnham said these ambitions are "connected to our long-term relationship with our nearest partner".

He said over the coming months before the next UK-EU summit both sides will "seek to conduct an informal brainstorming process" to look at what those options are.

Burnham added: "What we are announcing today is probably the limit of what we can achieve in this Parliament, given what needs to be done to implement it.

"But we want to inform the Parliament after this one, so that decisions can be taken about that long-term relationship."

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The prime minister said the "British people will be the boss of what happens next", with the next election needing to be held by August 2029.

Von der Leyen, who said the summit was a "turning point" for the EU-UK relationship, said "we are open for brainstorming".

Badenoch said the deal was not fair because UK farmers would now have to follow rules made by Brussels and more young people will come to the UK to "compete with our young people who are struggling to get jobs".

Badenoch was also asked about the Treasury's analysis, which Burnham said suggested the deal "will add up to 0.5%" to UK gross domestic product and he described it as a "Â£17bn boost to the British economy".

She claimed Burnham has "plucked that figure out of the air" and "the only modelling I've seen that even talks about that figure says it's actually 0.0%".

Farage said Friday was a "very good day for the EU", adding: "A good day to try and humiliate the one member state that actually had the courage to say, 'We don't need to be part of you, we can govern our own country'."

In a social media post, Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, added: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud. Only Reform will stop the endless betrayal of the British people."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."

#175. Tokenized stocks face pushback as NYSE, Nasdaq pivot for a new market

OKXICE, a trading platform for tokenized securities jointly operated by Intercontinental Exchange and crypto exchange OKX, is set to debut Monday despite objections from 90% of the companies whose stocks it initially proposed to list.

Of the 62 companies whose shares OKXICE first sought to trade, 56 objected to having their shares tokenized and traded on the platform, according to a review of public notices posted between Oct. 4 and Nov. 5. By last week, even more companies had objected, including those not on the original roster.

The high objection rate may reflect companies' lack of readiness to have blockchain-based representations of their shares traded on a new third-party platform like OKXICE, rather than broad resistance to tokenization.

People involved in talks with some of the companies say a 30-day objection window left them little time to react and decide if they were ready to participate.

"The shift to a 24/7 global marketplace is revolutionary and transformational for our market structure," Andrew Cuomo, co-chair of OKXICE, told CNBC. "The majority of companies aren't familiar with [tokenization] and haven't had a chance to understand it and the consequences."

"Logic dictates that many companies were going to be cautious before they understood what the system would do," added Cuomo, who served 10 years as governor of New York until 2021. "What you're seeing now is the more the companies learn about 24/7 global access, the more receptive they are."

At launch, tokenized shares of Coinbase, Robinhood, Strategy, Circle, Securitize and BitGo will be available to trade on the platform, OKXICE disclosed in a public notice. OKXICE has confirmed 113 assets, including exchange-traded funds and products, going live on a rolling basis. Since its initial pre-launch announcement on Nov. 11, OKXICE said it's seen further interest from different issuers and Fidelity Investments, Direxion, eToro, Strive and Vida Global are now participating.

The 30-day opt-out window is a requirement of the SEC's so-called Innovation Exemption, which took effect in September and temporarily permits the trading of certain tokenized stocks. Under the exemption, trading platforms must notify the underlying issuers and give them an opportunity to object. Any issuer who objects blocks its shares from being tokenized.

"We've gotten a lot of feedback that the reason why you've seen this high number of rejections of tokenizing equity is because it was a 30-day window," Seth Ginns, chief investment officer at Franklin Templeton's crypto arm, told CNBC at the Digital Assets Forum in New York earlier this month. Many issuers were open to exploring the idea but were hesitant to move forward amid competing priorities, he said.

"[They're] saying it's earnings season, there was the midterms, there's a lot going on during this 30-day window. Give us some time, we want to learn about it, we're not closed to the idea we're just closed to rushing into it right now," Ginns added.

Ran Grushkowsky, CEO of MassPay, a global payout platform, said several of his company's clients were among the initial targets and shared that their concern was less about the concept of tokenization and more about how 24/7 trading could affect stock value and investor relations strategies, particularly around the timing of corporate announcements.

"It's more [about] what's going to be the effect, how is it going to actually drive the value of the stock," he said. "A lot of the strategy that happens around investors relations has to do with scheduling and when you announce certain things and so how do you do it when your stock is being traded 24/7?"

"The executives were actually quite excited about this new innovation and potentially getting more interest into the stock. It was really from the [investor relations] side that they received push back," and heard "let's hold off," Grushkowsky added.

The lack of awareness may even extend to executives overseeing digital asset initiatives, suggesting that some rejections stem from internal communication gaps and unfamiliarity with the process, according to Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo, whose company is one of the first being listed on OKX. Securitize is also the tokenization partner for OKX.

"We've talked to some of the issuers that have opted out [where] the head of digital assets doesn't even know who in the company rejected them," he said. "This is a consequence of, first, the novelty of the process and of these venues, and second, the lack of understanding of what they're being notified to do."

Despite the initial pushback, Domingo expects more issuers to participate as companies become more familiar with the model, noting that some regulated trading venues can operate without issuer consent.

Company objections raise a separate question about which of the different tokenization models will prove most attractive. The main alternative to the OKXICE model is an issuer-led approach that integrates blockchain-based shares into existing regulated market infrastructures, with issuers retaining greater control over the process.

Bullish, a digital asset platform, is pursuing an example of this model through its partnership with Equiniti, the transfer-agent business it's acquiring for $4 billion.

Nasdaq's planned launch of an issuer-centric tokenized equities model in partnership with Kraken parent Payward in the second quarter of next year could offer a test of that framework and whether companies are more willing to embrace tokenization under a different structure.

Nasdaq and the New York Stock Exchange, owned by Intercontinental, and others are responding to a structural shift in how securities are issued, traded and settled. Tokenization has become attractive to institutions and investors because it enables round the clock stock trading, shortened settlement times, fractional ownership and eases the use of securities as collateral.

For the exchanges, it presents a way to keep control of trading and market infrastructure as crypto-native, blockchain-based venues seek to bring traditional financial assets on to blockchain. It's a long way off before it becomes a meaningful revenue driver, however, according to Clear Street analyst Owen Lau.

"It increases the [total addressable market], that's the number one thing," Lau said. "In the near term, there may not be enough of an incremental revenue opportunity, but the trend is there. The trend is to tokenize equities, the trend is to facilitate 24/7 trading."

#174. MP criticises Guernsey and Jersey company ownership register plans

MP Sir Andrew Mitchell has campaigned for publicly accessible registers of who owns companies in British Overseas Territories and Crown Dependencies

Plans to improve transparency over company ownership in Guernsey and Jersey have come under criticism from a Westminster MP, who says they "do not go nearly far enough".

The comments come after Guernsey, external and Jersey, external set out plans to allow approved applicants access to information about who owns or controls companies when investigating suspected financial crime.

Sir Andrew Mitchell, who has campaigned for greater scrutiny of company ownership, said there were "immediate indications" the proposals would fall short.

He said limits on access to the information could mean "national security threats may remain entirely invisible to investigators".

The plans follow a joint commitment made by Guernsey, Jersey and the Isle of Man in December 2023 to increase transparency around company ownership and help tackle money laundering, terrorist financing and other financial crime.

Currently, information about who ultimately owns or controls companies is generally only available to law enforcement agencies, regulators and certain regulated businesses carrying out anti-money laundering checks.

The new proposals would allow journalists, academics and other applicants with a legitimate interest to apply for access.

Sir Andrew said investigators could face a "catch-22" because they may need access to the register to prove wrongdoing in the first place.

He also said he understood applicants would have to show they had been unable to find the information elsewhere, arguing: "This entirely defeats the object of a register."

Jersey's consultation response says applicants would be expected to show that "all reasonable alternative sources have been exhausted" before access was granted.

Guernsey's published response does not explicitly set out the same requirement.

He said: "Access should be open to those from other jurisdictions, for example those in the global south from whom much of the world's dirty money was stolen in the first place."

Sir Andrew also questioned whether users would have to pay to access the information, saying: "This information should be free at the point of access."

He said the proposals should eventually lead to fully public registers.

Both the governments of Guernsey and Jersey rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and said they continue to meet international standards in tackling financial crime.

A Government of Jersey spokesperson said the concerns raised by him "do not accurately reflect what is in Jersey's consultation response paper".

They said the policy was intended to help Jersey meet its international commitments while maintaining "a fair, proportionate, and effective transparency framework".

They added: "Jersey continues to meet international standards for transparency and countering money laundering, terrorist financing and proliferation financing."

Deputy Andrew Niles, from Guernsey's Policy & Resources Committee, rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and pointed to a positive 2025 assessment of the island's efforts to tackle financial crime, external.

He said: "Guernsey does not accept the suggestion that our proposals fall short of the commitments we've made on beneficial ownership transparency or that the island does not meet international standards on beneficial ownership transparency."

"The proposal is based on a tried and tested model adopted in the EU," he added.

#171. CrowdStrike to buy Zscaler in $44 billion deal as AI supercharges cyberattacks

CrowdStrike plans to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in an all-stock deal that combines two significant security businesses at a time when artificial intelligence is supercharging attacks.

Shares of CrowdStrike dropped 9% on Monday, while Zscaler surged nearly 18%.

The deal is expected to close in the second half of CrowdStrike's fiscal year 2028. Under the agreement, Zscaler shareholders will receive 1.135 CrowdStrike shares for each share.

CrowdStrike's acquisition pushes the Austin, Texas-based cyber vendor into the network security market and comes amid a massive year for cybersecurity deal-making in the wake of high-profile hacks like Hugging Face.

"With Zscaler, we're building cybersecurity's operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely," CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said in a statement. "We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen."

Recent outages affecting Microsoft, the Federal Aviation Administration and JPMorgan Chase have sparked anxiety as businesses accelerate software patching and upgrades to aging systems in an effort to prevent bad actors from exploiting software vulnerabilities with AI.

CrowdStrike has been a major beneficiary of skyrocketing demand for AI security, with shares soaring about 80% this year as of Monday's open. Zscaler shares are up about 5% year to date after Monday's surge. Their combined market value exceeded $265 billion as of Friday's close, behind market leader Palo Alto Networks at $282 billion.

The deal marks CrowdStrike's biggest acquisition to date and is among the largest in the sector as businesses consolidate and ramp up defenses to fend off AI-powered attacks.

Last year, Google announced plans to acquire Wiz for $32 billion. In 2024 Cisco shelled about $28 billion for Splunk. Competitor Palo Alto Networks has also spent billions on several cyber and AI businesses, including startup Applied Compute and a $25 billion deal for CyberArk, which it closed earlier this year.

Analysts view the Zscaler deal as a major AI security opportunity, allowing CrowdStrike to capture more customer security spending and close a significant gap with Palo Alto.

"The addition of ZS to CRWD's platform could bring more breadth in an era of vendor consolidation and, more importantly, network security to position the company more effectively for rising demand for AI Sovereignty," wrote analysts at JPMorgan.

CrowdStrike on Monday also reported better-than-expected fiscal third-quarter results, including earnings of 34 cents per share on $1.58 billion in revenue. That topped the 31 cents per share on $1.53 billion in revenue forecast by LSEG.

Zscaler's fiscal first-quarter earnings, also released on Monday, likewise surpassed estimates.

#170. Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

The prime minister made a lot of the UK's new deal with the EU cutting costs for business and consumers during tough times.

In another world, Andy Burnham would have loved for the nation's supermarkets to clearly announce that they could cut food prices as a result of this latest agreement on food exports.

And while grocers have very much welcomed Friday's announcement, they have notably not committed to price cuts as a direct result of this move.

The managing director of M&S, Alex Freudmann, called the deal a "major step forward", saying it would remove bureaucracy. Tesco chief executive Ken Murphy added it was "good news for British farmers and producers".

Freudmann highlighted some of hoops that were hurting UK-EU trade, using the example that "getting our spaghetti Bolognese from Lincolnshire onto a shelf in Dublin takes hundreds of extra miles, mountains of paperwork, a vet's signature and even the Latin name of a cow".

Such measures will no longer need to be taken once this deal kicks in. Good news for business exports then, but will that lead to lower shopping bills here in the UK?

This deal will matter for food exporters - it may not have a clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders say there are too many moving parts in the world economy, especially given the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

It is more a hope than will at this stage. The industry and government hope that the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers.

This is not the first time Labour has sought to reduce the weekly shop costs. Sir Keir Starmer's administration floated the idea that supermarkets might even offer some form of branded discounts, a suggestion that was met with collective bemusement by those in the industry.

However, there will be less paperwork, checks and red tape on food imports too, but they were already lightly enforced.

No longer will hundreds of pages of documents be required. No longer will perishable goods lose valuable hours or days at border checks. No longer will the incorrect colour of ink or Latin name for a meat disallow a shipment of British exports.

Some, though not all of the 24% hit since 2019 to the real value of agrifood exports, could be regained by a bonfire of this red tape.

In particular, smaller exporters of food will be able to return to the large EU market.

But the extra benefit here is unclear, and there could be extra checks and costs required instead on non-EU food imports.

The government's own assessment is that the economy will gain Â£5bn through this, or 0.14% by 2040.

Critics point out that this benefit does not include the quid pro quo - having to align with EU laws. Farmers have pointed to compliance with pesticides and fungicide regulation.

The classic example of the benefits of post-Brexit autonomy has been the development of precision-bred foodstuffs, such as Vitamin D-enhanced tomatoes.

The producer of this technology has, however, backed the deal after the government negotiated an "exception period" for ongoing UK sale.

Professor Cristobal Uauy of the John Innes Centre said that "aligning with the EU market will bring our science to a greater number of people".

Bringing all the agreements on food, emissions and the youth experience together, the economy is forecast to get an overall boost of 0.5%, or Â£17bn a year.

A modest boost, but high by the standards of past post-Brexit trade deals, for example, with Australia, India or Japan and the Gulf and the trans-pacific deal.

This UK-EU pact actually matches all of those agreements combined for expected growth measured by the Treasury's own assessments.

The biggest single boost to the overall economy would come from more European young workers. However, the economy benefit of this part of the deal per person reduces to zero, according to the government's own calculations.

Burham also said he hoped a planned electricity deal with the EU would cut bills, but that has not been done yet, and requires a contribution into the bloc's budget for poorer regions.

The electricity deal could help lower bills, but that contains a clear trade-off.

There are still some details to be revealed in the full legal texts of these documents.

The bigger point is whether this logic of gaining free-flowing access for food, in exchange for giving up regulatory freedom, will now apply elsewhere.

Is food now a blueprint for future negotiations? It had been the Starmer-Rachel Reeves strategy to spread this model to other sectors such as cars, and chemicals.

The big takeaway from today was that both the PM and his counterparts publicly acknowledged the end of the incremental piecemeal approach to aligning with the single market.

The question is whether the PM thinks what goes for food applies across the whole economy. Most economists predict that could lead to much bigger economic gains, but it will also be a much bigger constitutional battle.

#169. Walmart hikes outlook as CEO tells CNBC retailer aims to keep prices low despite soaring fuel costs

Walmart on Thursday reported earnings and revenue that narrowly topped estimates and raised its full-year outlook as it saw strong e-commerce growth ahead of the busy holiday season.

Walmart said global e-commerce sales surged 17% in the fiscal third quarter, driven by store-fulfilled pickup and delivery, with Walmart U.S. up 23% and Sam's Club U.S. rising 30%. Its Walmart U.S. advertising business saw a 37% sales increase for the quarter.

Walmart said it rolled back prices on nearly 8,000 products across its stores, a focus as U.S. consumers worry about the cost of living in the face of elevated food and gas prices.

CEO John Furner told CNBC's Becky Quick that the company has seen higher-than-expected diesel costs, but that tariff refunds have helped the retailer to offset those inputs and maintain lower prices.

"The reality is that increases in diesel fuel and transportation, they do work their way into cost of goods," Furner said in an exclusive interview. "So we're hoping to see these costs come down as soon as possible, and we'll keep prices as low as we can."

CFO John David Rainey told CNBC that growth in online orders for Walmart and Sam's Club was driven by an emphasis on speed of delivery and reduced prices. He added that Walmart saw its largest share gains in the food category in three years during the third quarter.

"This was another really solid quarter for us, we feel good about it," Rainey told CNBC. "We continue to gain share while continuing to improve the profitability of our digital businesses."

Walmart also raised its full-year guidance, now expecting net sales to increase between 4.9% and 5.2%, compared with previous guidance of a 4% to 5% increase. Rainey said Walmart is maintaining "flexibility" in its guidance.

Walmart anticipates adjusted earnings per share will come in between $2.84 and $2.89, compared with a prior range of $2.80 to $2.87. It's also now expecting adjusted operating income to increase between 8% and 8.6%, up from the previous outlook of between 7% and 8.5% growth.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, the company reported net income of $4.95 billion, or 62 cents per share, down from $6.14 billion, or 77 cents per share, a year earlier. Revenue rose 5.2%.

Adjusting for investment gains and losses, tax matters and reorganization charges, Walmart earned 65 cents per share.

Walmart U.S. recorded net sales of $125 billion, up 3.6% from a year earlier. Its comparable sales excluding fuel rose 2.9%, in line with Wall Street expectations, according to StreetAccount.

Furner also said Walmart is seeing encouraging adoption of its artificial intelligence agent, named Sparky, especially ahead of the holiday season. He said it's led to customer basket increases of 40% on average.

"It just is a great way of just completing the entire basket and solving entire the solution for people," he said.

Sam's Club reported net sales of $25.9 billion, marking a 10% increase from the year-ago period. It said its sales were driven by strength in the grocery category and general merchandise.

In October, Walmart announced it was launching a joint membership option with Walmart+ and Sam's Club as it leans further into convenience. Both stores will still offer stand-alone memberships, as well.

Furner told CNBC the new membership has brought roughly 2 million new people into Sam's Club since its launch in late October. Though the company reimbursed some members for the new membership rate, Rainey added that it's already seeing positive returns on the joint model and that consumers are "spending appreciably more."

The company on Thursday said its global membership fee revenue grew 17% in the third quarter.

The company is typically well insulated from macroeconomic pressures because of its value reputation. Last quarter, the company said it was lowering prices of products across multiple categories, including beef.

Rainey said Thursday that he's "optimistic" about consumer spending heading into the holiday season and that the fourth quarter is already "off to a pretty good start."

The big-box retailer has posted solid growth over the past few quarters as it sees customers gravitate to reduced prices, improvements in delivery and gains in its advertising business. It's also leaned into attracting higher-income consumers with its Walmart+ membership.

Shares of Walmart have fluctuated this year but are up roughly 13% year to date.

#166. Amazon to acquire AI startup Decart's chip software, hire founders

Amazon is scooping up much of the technology and talent behind artificial intelligence startup Decart, marking the latest reverse acquihire deal by a major tech company.

In a Monday blog post, Amazon said it agreed to purchase Decart's "proprietary technology" and hire the startup's three founders, as well as their AI researchers and engineers.

The Israeli startup, founded in 2023, is best known for its software that lets developers sync their AI models across different chips, allowing them to switch hardware quickly, known as the Decart Optimization Stack.

"Every leap forward in AI starts with getting more out of every chip," Peter DeSantis, a senior vice president at Amazon who oversees foundation models, custom silicon and quantum computing development, said in a statement. "More efficient compute means better training, better reinforcement learning, faster inference, and better models for customers at a lower cost."

DeSantis added, "From our earliest work with Decart, we knew they were the kind of technologically fearless team who would help us push it even further."

Amazon has moved deeper into building custom AI chips amid insatiable demand for the technology, and as companies seek alternatives to Nvidia, the dominant provider. Amazon has positioned its Trainium and Inferentia chips as lower-cost options for AI developers.

Last month, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy said the company plans to make its Trainium chips externally available for the first time in "late in 2027."

Several outlets reported Amazon will pay $7.5 billion for Decart's assets, citing people familiar with the matter. Amazon declined to comment on the terms of the deal.

Anthropic had considered acquiring Decart and conducted due diligence, but ultimately walked away from a deal, Bloomberg reported.

Tech companies including Microsoft, Meta and Google have announced a series of deals with buzzy AI startups, which involve licensing their technology or nabbing their top talent and founders. The moves, often referred to as reverse acquihires, stop short of buying the companies outright. Amazon made two similar deals in 2024 when it brought on talent from Adept and Covariant.

The arrangements have attracted regulatory scrutiny in some cases. Amazon said Monday the Decart deal is subject to regulatory approval.

#165. Italian jeweller and businessman Paolo Bulgari dies aged 89

Italian jeweller Paolo Bulgari, who helped turn his family's business into a global luxury brand, has died in Rome aged age 89.

His family announced the news, remembering him as "the greatest jeweller of our time", according to Italian media.

Paolo Bulgari was Chairman of the Bvlgari Group since 1984 and its jewellery pieces have adorned Hollywood stars and royalty including Diana, Princess of Wales.

The company Bvlgari posted an online tribute to the "true creative soul", whose work would "continue to inspire generations to come".

The firm, Bulgari, was set up by his grandfather, Greek silversmith Sotirios Voulgaris, in Rome in the late 19th century - who later adapted his name to Bulgari in Italian.

Originally from Paramythia, a mountain village in the Epirus region of northwestern Greece renowned for its silver jewellery, Voulgaris fled Ottoman rule and settled in Naples before opening a shop in Rome.

Paolo Bulgari joined the company shortly after leaving school, becoming an expert in gemstones - eventually leading the business and overseeing international growth.

The Bulgari family favoured bold gemstones, rich yellow gold, generous proportions and unexpected colour combinations to set them apart from the delicate asethetic associated with Parisian jewellery houses.

The business broke into luxury markets and diversified beyond jewellery into watches, perfumes, leather goods and hotels.

"Paolo Bulgari began collaborating with his father in 1960, specialising in the knowledge of precious stones, of which he was one of the world's leading experts, and refining the sensibility and aesthetic sense that are at the root of the Bvlgari style," the Bvlgari company posted on social media., external

"He poured his heart into his craft and into nurturing the talents of young designers, leaving an indelible mark on the world of jewellery.

"Thank you, Mr. Paolo, your legacy as a brilliant beacon of creative inspiration will continue to inspire generations to come."

Paolo Bulgari alongside David Bowie and his wife Iman in September 1991 in Versailles, France.

The post included a quote from Paolo Bulgari: "Everything can be a source of inspiration. There are no rules in creativity. Never."

The designer brand's celebrity clients have included Elizabeth Taylor, Audrey Hepburn, Marilyn Monroe, Ingrid Bergman, Sophie Loren, Anne Hathaway, Zendaya and Rihanna.

Actor Zendaya wearing a Bvlgari necklace at the 2025 Golden Globe awards.

In 2011 the family sold its controlling stake to the French luxury group LVMH, in a deal worth about 3.7 billion euros.

Chairman and CEO of LVMH, Bernard Arnault - who is one of the world's richest people - offered his condolences and also paid tribute to Paolo Bulgari.

"Throughout his life, he was a key architect of the brand's success, while simultaneously upholding and embodying the Italian spirit and the deeply Roman heritage of our exceptional jewellery Maison," he said in a statement to the BBC.

In 2019, Paolo Bulgari and his wife established the Paolo Bulgari Foundation to support young people through schooling and job training in disadvantaged neighbourhoods on the outskirts of Rome.

The funeral will take place in Rome on Monday, 23 November in the Basilica of Santa Croce in Gerusalemme.

#164. Can AI really help spot Black Friday deals? We put it to the test

Whether it's scrolling through endless choice, spotting fake reviews, or working out if a "deal" is a deal at all, Black Friday sums up a lot of online woes.

Retailers promise bargains - but the sheer number of offers can leave us feeling no closer to finding the best deal.

It's an area where generative AI products, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini, have promised to help.

But are they any good at finding Black Friday deals? We had a go ourselves a week ahead of the big day.

Figures suggest relatively few UK shoppers currently go to AI tools for help.

A recent YouGov survey of 19 countries found the UK ranked second-last in the proportion of people who said they had used AI to help them shop.

Crucially, 36% of respondents who said they would be shopping around Black Friday didn't plan to use an AI tool at all.

But the survey suggested a growing number of people were considering it - and tech firms have released features to capitalise on this.

Amazon launched Alexa for Shopping for UK users in November and OpenAI launched a Shopping Research tool on ChatGPT days before last year's Black Friday.

In the US, Google recently launched Universal Cart, with AI-powered features such as price tracking and identification of relevant promo codes.

But there has been criticism that fact-checking AI ends up taking more time, while some brands are working to try to make sure their products show up in chatbot answers.

We asked four models to find a 55-inch TV deal, using the free versions of many of the most popular tools.

While this was not a rigorous scientific test, the experience we had produced mixed results.

OpenAI's ChatGPT came up with four options.

Its top pick was a Hisense TV on Chinese retailer Joybuy's website, on sale for Â£499 down from Â£649.

The next recommendation was a 55-inch TCL-branded TV, but a sidebar showed prices for the 50-inch version, from Â£379 to Â£814.

Option three was the same Hisense TV as before, but for full price at Â£649 from John Lewis and Richer Sounds.

This was the "best safer purchase" according to ChatGPT, as the sites offered longer guarantee periods.

But clicking on the links did not go well. Both retailers said the TV was no longer listed online.

The fourth option was equally fruitless - another TCL TV, at full price, Â£150 over our budget and not available online.

At the bottom, ChatGPT served one last offer - "one OLED option to compare" - for a Â£1,100 LG TV that was marked as "Ad".

Claude produced the shortest answer of all the models we tested, and unlike the others, it did not give much detail on the specifications of each model.

Instead, it seemed to base its results on a small number of reviews published online.

It recommended the same two TCL TVs as ChatGPT.

But when we clicked on the links, they sent us to two dead pages on the Richer Sounds website.

Some other links led to year-old round-ups of Black Friday 2025 deals.

Claude was not very enthusiastic about its other two recommendations due to a lack of independent reviews - calling one "a decent option" but saying it "wouldn't pick" the other.

It also listed two Samsung TVs, but it told us not to buy them, with one "over budget and not clearly better than the TCLs".

We were left wondering why it even mentioned them in the first place.

Different AI model, same TV manufacturers - albeit different Hisense and TCL models to the previous chatbots' recommendations.

Gemini generated a table comparing four different models, but offered no links to the stores it named, so we searched for them ourselves.

The prices for one TCL TV were correct at Â£649, but it was sold out at one of the named retailers.

It said the other TCL model was available from Argos and Currys for Â£539 - correct for Argos, but when we checked Currys, it was Â£60 more expensive.

Again, it included two Samsung models to avoid due to lower screen specs.

Alexa came back with four TVs, from its own Amazon Ember at Â£532 to a Â£699 Samsung.

But its top recommendation was to go for another Samsung model at Â£616 (down from Â£749), based on its picture quality and Amazon customer rating.

Its fourth option was for a Sony TV, but it was only 50 inches, leaving us unsure why it had included it.

All the prices stated were correct, but Amazon only searches its own store. Alexa did acknowledge this, encouraging us to check its rivals for better deals, "as prices can vary" and we could get longer guarantees elsewhere.

There are methods that can boost the likelihood of getting a better response, from crafting more informative prompts to upping a chatbot's "thinking" level.

Tech journalist Alex Hughes had better luck than we did when he used ChatGPT's agentic AI mode to find a TV in last year's Black Friday sales.

Agentic AI is still in its early days, but it allows AI to act with more autonomy and carry out multi-step tasks.

The search took about 20 minutes "with very little work on my end needed," Hughes wrote for Tom's Guide, external in November 2025.

A year on, he told the BBC he didn't go through with buying a TV at that time.

He did later purchase one of the seven models ChatGPT had surfaced - but he had found it for a lower price in a bricks-and-mortar shop.

Will he be using AI to find Black Friday deals again this year? "Probably not," he said.

He reckoned it saved him time, but "when you're trying to deal with something like live price data... it will be inconsistent and quite often incorrect".

While we were served up the same brands again and again, in reality there are tonnes of products listed for sale around Black Friday.

Naureen Mohammed, who advises businesses on how to get their products to appear in AI results, told the BBC customers did not see the full picture when they talked to chatbots.

If businesses fail to update their prices when people start hunting for bargains, "last year's prices are served up as today's deals".

"I suspect the whole model for advertising is going to start influencing AI recommendations," she said.

Her own analysis of chatbots' Black Friday tech deals in the UK suggested the big retailers came out on top - with Amazon mentioned in half of responses, and Currys following closely behind.

Her tips include asking an assistant "is that this year's price?" and clicking on the links provided - "because you shouldn't take any advice from the AI system at face value".

But consumer group Which? recently said inaccurate pricing in AI results added more work, external to Black Friday deal-hunting.

"If you ask ChatGPT for a list of 10 laptops under Â£400 and seven of the results are priced higher, you've likely wasted a good chunk of time going through them to discover the bot gave you only three options," said senior researcher Hannah Walsh.

"The opposite is also true - you could miss out on great models in your budget if ChatGPT tells you they are more expensive than they actually are."

Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

Listen: Black Friday - are you really saving money?

#161. SpaceX sued by Oregon and Virginia over AI deepfake porn, Grok safety claims

Lawsuits against Elon Musk's SpaceX are stacking up, with Virginia and Oregon becoming the first states to sue the company over issues with its Grok AI image generator.

Versions of Grok and SpaceX's social network X have enabled users to generate and distribute explicit deepfakes based on real photos or videos of children and other people who did not consent, according to research by the Internet Watch Foundation, the Center for Countering Digital Hate and others.

In separate complaints submitted to state courts as of Monday morning, Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield and Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones argued that SpaceX failed to put safeguards in place to stop Grok and X users from making and sharing harmful deepfake images, especially alleged AI-generated child sexual abuse material.

The complaints also allege that SpaceX has falsely represented its AI apps and social network (Grok and X) as generally safe, which the states see as a violation of Virginia's Consumer Protection Act or Oregon's Unlawful Trade Practices Act. The matter is particularly concerning because X allows minors 13 and older to access its content.

Rayfield and Jones both noted in their complaints that harms caused by non-consensual, sexualized images go beyond the victims depicted in the images.

In a statement emailed to CNBC by a spokesperson for SpaceXSI, and posted on the company's @Safety account on X, the company said, "When someone tries to break our safeguards and generate this content, we take appropriate measures, including actioning accounts and notifying law enforcement. Attempting to create this material is a serious crime, and the individuals who do it should be prosecuted to the fullest extent of the law. We will continue to work with the authorities via all available means to pursue these bad actors."

At an NYC Council hearing in October, a SpaceX AI policy leader, Gabriela Castillo Madrid, said the company had appropriate "safeguards" and "strict controls" in place around its AI products.

SpaceX was already facing multiple Grok- and X-related lawsuits, and government probes around the world, including by authorities in the U.K., Spain, Ireland, France and the state of California.

In private-sector lawsuits filed against SpaceX in the U.S. concerning Grok and AI-generated, explicit images, litigation is still in early stages and is tracked by The Center for Internet Technology Addiction.

When the city of Baltimore sued xAI, now owned and operated by SpaceX, the company argued that under Section 230, they were not liable for unwanted images. Section 230 is a federal law that largely shields online platforms from liability for users' content.

SpaceX also sued a user in a federal court in the Northern District of Texas for breach of contract after he allegedly used Grok to create and distribute deepfake explicit images, including depictions of minors. Lawyers for Musk's company argued that Grok should be seen as "a neutral tool, subject to user control," and that any image generation "is the result of the user's prompts and directions."

The new state suits add to legal pressure on SpaceX. Both Virginia and Oregon are seeking injunctions, civil penalties, disgorgement and legal costs, while Virginia is also seeking restitution for consumers from Musk's company.

Oregon submitted its complaint to the Multnomah County Circuit Court on Sunday evening, and Virginia submitted its complaint to the City of Richmond Circuit Court Monday morning. As of early Monday, the suits were pending the courts' acceptance.

State attorneys general have recently succeeded in compelling U.S. social media companies to change their products and pay for alleged past harms.

In August, for example, Meta settled a major federal case centered on allegations that the social media giant misrepresented the level of child mental health problems caused by its apps, Facebook and Instagram. Without admitting to wrongdoing, Meta agreed to pay states around $17 billion over a 10-year period and to make app changes that experts believe will help keep children safer online.

#159. Gap names new Athleta CEO, says Old Navy turnaround is seeing signs of progress

Gap Inc. on Thursday announced Ashley Kechter will be the next president and CEO of its Athleta brand, effective Dec. 14.

The retailer also said Old Navy's turnaround is showing signs of progress, sending shares up more than 11% Friday.

Kechter comes to Gap from activewear retailer Vuori, where she was global president and oversaw the brand's global business strategy, marketing and financials. She was previously global brand president of Fabletics and began her career at Gap before that.

"I'm excited to return to Gap Inc. and partner with [Gap CEO Richard Dickson] and the team to strengthen our connection with customers and shape the brand's next chapter," Kechter said in a statement.

The announcement comes after Lululemon poached former Athleta CEO Maggie Gauger to be its chief product officer last month.

"Clearly, we're disappointed with Athleta's performance, and we recognize there's a lot of work ahead," Dickson told CNBC. "We had been in rebuild mode, but now we're really in turnaround mode."

Dickson said the company is focused on getting back to its fundamentals: fabrications, feature, form, function and fit. Dickson added that Kechter will be conducting a "comprehensive review" of the brand to reposition Athleta for future success.

While the execution of its strategy has thus far "fallen short," Dickson said this new approach will allow Athleta to fully realize its potential.

Gap also reported mixed fiscal third-quarter earnings on Thursday and posted flat overall comparable sales. Wall Street had been expecting a comparable sales increase of 1.3%, according to StreetAccount.

Net sales grew 2%, though demand softened in October and early November due to warmer weather, the company said. Store sales increased 1%, while online sales increased 3%.

Gap also said it's recorded a $420 million adjustment to cost of goods sold and a $21 million adjustment to interest income from tariff refunds so far this year.

The company lowered its full-year net sales outlook to be "roughly flat," down from previous guidance of up between 1% and 1.5%. Gap is also now expecting adjusted earnings per share of between $2.29 and $2.34, down from an earlier expectation of between $2.35 and $2.45 per share.

Dickson said in an interview that much of the lowered guidance was due to warmer weather in the first two weeks of the quarter, but he said Gap is expecting "meaningful acceleration" in shopping ahead of the peak holiday season.

"We're monitoring the trends, and we're going to remain agile as we respond to the changing business trends," Dickson said.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter, Gap reported net income of $311 million, or 86 cents per share, compared with $236 million, or 62 cents per share, the year prior. Sales rose slightly from $3.94 billion the year prior.

At Athleta, net sales were down 14% compared with last year, coming in at $220 million. Comparable sales for the banner sank 15%, which the company attributed to "limited traction" from efforts to modernize its merchandise and marketing. `

The company's namesake brand saw 9% comparable sales growth for the quarter, with net sales of $1.1 billion, marking an 11% increase compared with last year. Dickson said it was the brand's 12th consecutive quarter of positive comparable sales.

"Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency of the brand is really giving us confidence in the brand's ability to continue its momentum," Dickson said.

The Old Navy banner saw flat comparable sales for the quarter and net sales of $2.3 billion, up 1% compared with last year. It's the first earnings report since the brand, which has been experiencing sluggish sales, named Michael Francis as its new CEO last quarter. Francis began his role at Old Navy on Nov. 2.

Banana Republic saw its net sales sink 3% compared with last year, which the company attributed to delayed demand for cold-weather categories and a women's fall assortment that fell flat. Wall Street had been expecting growth of 1.5%, according to StreetAccount.

Dickson said the brand has since introduced new products and seen demand "rebound" as cooler weather has set in across the country.

"We've been encouraged by the early customer response, and we should see sequential improvement in the fourth quarter," he said. "There's some work to do here, but with peak selling still ahead, I'm confident that the team is focused on the right areas and [will] ultimately position Banana well for growth."
