#158. Centrica boss says he is 'hugely concerned' over impact of soaring energy bills

The boss of British Gas owner Centrica says he is "hugely concerned" about the impact the expected jump in energy bills in January will have on consumers.

Chris O'Shea told the BBC he thought the number of people unable or unwilling to pay their energy bills would rise when prices go up in the new year.

The new energy price cap to take effect from January will be announced on Wednesday and consultancy Cornwall Insight has forecast it will jump by Â£443 a year for a typical household.

The government has said some households will get Â£100 to help with bills, but one Citizens Advice adviser said it was a "drop in the ocean" while a pressure group called for action on "inflated energy firm profits".

Speaking to the Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme, O'Shea said the predictions for a "substantial increase" in bills from January were "materially correct", adding that the first quarter of the year was the time when most energy was consumed.

"That's right at the point where people will be paying for Christmas and the like. So I am hugely concerned," he said.

O'Shea added there was a "massive problem" with people not paying their bills in the UK, with some not able to pay their bills and an "increasing number of people that are choosing not to pay their bills".

He said the collective impact of that was that "every single electricity consumer, is paying about Â£1.50, Â£1.60 a week to cover the cost of people that are not paying their bills, and it's just going to get worse when bills go up".

The amount owed to energy suppliers by households hit Â£5bn in the April to June period, according to energy regulator Ofgem.

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Typical energy bill forecast to go up by Â£443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

Wholesale energy prices paid by suppliers have been rising this year as a result of the conflict in the Middle East, which has pushed up bills for households.

Cornwall Insight has predicted Ofgem's energy price cap will rise by 26% to Â£2,166 a year for a typical household from 1 January.

The price cap affects about 20 million households in England, Scotland and Wales and sets a maximum price for each unit of gas and electricity, so the actual level of bills will vary according to energy usage.

The government has said some households will get a Â£100 payment to help with energy bills, a policy which was announced in the Budget.

The government has also temporarily cut 5% VAT from electricity bills until the end of June next year, which is expected to save the typical household about Â£45 a year.

But consumer groups have argued the Â£100 payment announced in the Budget will not be enough for some.

One Citizens Advice adviser said it was a "drop in the ocean" and that we would see "cold-related deaths" this winter.

"For someone in a lot of debt, who can't afford their bills, it's not going to touch the sides."

The Fuel Poverty Action Group said the government and Ofgem needed to take stronger action against energy companies.

"Our inflated energy prices are driven by inflated energy firm profits," the group said.

"Government and Ofgem must take stronger action against this blatant profiteering whilst we suffer in cold homes, or are forced into debt."

Energy firms operating in the UK are subject to a windfall tax that was introduced in 2022.

When the windfall tax is added to other taxes levied, it means the overall tax rate on profits earned by oil and gas companies operating in the UK is 78%.

#155. Hundreds of applications, no replies: Inside the job ghosting problem‌

Ffion, Yugraj and Jessica say being ghosted has become a common part of applying for jobs

Have you ever applied for a job or attended an interview, only to hear absolutely nothing back from the employer?

So-called "job ghosting" has become a big problem for jobseekers in the UK, leaving them "demoralised", denting their confidence and undermining their hopes of finding work.

It's an issue that was mentioned in former minister Alan Milburn's major review into young people who are not in employment, education or training - those dubbed as Neets - this week.

Among his recommendations - aimed at reducing the proportion of Neets from 13% to about 5% by 2036 - Milburn proposed that large employers should give all unsuccessful job applicants under 25 "meaningful feedback".

The report's proposals relate mostly to England, with a recommendation that Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland adapt the principles to suit their own systems.

But is feedback for all applicants realistic, given the vast number of applications these firms receive? And what can people do to improve their chances in such a tough job market?

Yugraj Samrai, 23, from Coventry is currently unemployed and says he has submitted hundreds of job applications since he graduated from his marketing degree in July.

But 30 to 40% of the time he hears nothing back at all, which can make him "feel like a statistic, not a person".

He tells the BBC: "I understand there are very few graduate jobs available, but at least give me a response to say whether I'll progress onto the next stage or not."

Ffion Johns, from Llanelli, has been unemployed since June and did not get an interview until September after submitting 70 applications.

The 24-year-old says her biggest problem throughout the job-hunting process has been the lack of feedback about why she didn't get the job, so she knows what to fix next time.

Sometimes she has even been ghosted after attending an interview.

"With most of these roles I'm not getting a rejection. I'm just not hearing anything, which can be quite frustrating," she says.

Research by head-hunting firm Robert Walters last year found 86% of UK job hunters said their applications had been ignored, with no response at all from the hiring organisation.

The Milburn report, external says this "wall of silence" is especially hard on young people as it gives them "nothing to learn from". It adds that "time and emotional investment" goes into their applications.

"It can really take hours and hours, so it just feels even worse to not even have that be recognised," says Jessica Anderson, 21, from Swindon.

Employers say they are being flooded with job applications in a very competitive jobs market and it can be hard to respond to all of them.

And AI is adding to the problem, enabling applicants to submit large numbers of applications in the hope of improving their chances.

But Meg Burton, a career coach based in Leeds, says firms should at the very least inform job applicants when they are unsuccessful and that AI could help achieve this.

Candidates should also be given proper feedback if they reach the interview stage, she says.

"It doesn't have to be hugely detailed, it's just some pointers on why they were unsuccessful and what they can learn from that experience going forward," she says.

The government could choose to adopt the Milburn proposals and crack down on job ghosting.

But it raises another question: if candidates began receiving significantly more rejections than before, could this itself have a demoralising effect?

Ellie McCrone, 20, from Northampton understands the concern but thinks hearing back and getting constructive feedback where possible is always best.

Ellie says constructive feedback is always preferable to being ghosted

"If you are applying for hundreds of jobs and never getting anything, I think something is going wrong... You can't fix something unless you know what the problem is."

There are things applicants can do to improve their chances and avoid ghosting, such as tailoring their CV to each job and not submitting "hundreds and hundreds of applications" that all look the same, Burton adds.

Candidates should also never take being ghosted personally, she adds.

"Not hearing back doesn't mean you aren't a great candidate. Put it down to experience and move on."

#152. South East Water reports loss after supply issues

Thousands of customers were affected by supply disruptions during high temperatures in May

South East Water has reported a half-year loss after penalties and Â£29m of costs following disruption to water supplies over the summer.

The company, which serves about 2.4 million customers across south-east England, recorded a pre-tax loss of Â£22.9m in the six months to 30 September, compared with a profit of Â£18.2m during the same period last year.

In July, regulator Ofwat ordered the company to spend Â£30.5m on improvements after supply interruptions hit customers across Kent and Sussex which affected more than 286,000 customers.

The company spent Â£29m responding to further supply interruptions during summer heatwaves in Kent and Sussex.

This included providing bottled water, setting up emergency water stations and compensating customers.

South East Water said it had set aside Â£14.6m in its latest financial results to cover the redress package.

The company supplies water to customers in Kent, East and West Sussex, Surrey, Hampshire and Berkshire.

Hosepipe bans remain in force across all areas supplied by South East Water.

In October, it was forced to ask shareholders for Â£50m after warning earlier in the year that it required additional funding in order to stay afloat.

The company said there were "early signs of improvement" following changes to its leadership team and plans for a "significant transformation" of the business.

Earlier this year, John Halsall was appointed chief executive after former chief executive David Hinton and chair Chris Train stepped down.

The departures followed criticism from MPs who said they had "no confidence" in the company's leadership after a review of repeated supply outages.

Richard Morse is due to become chair of the company on 3 December, succeeding interim chair Lisa Clement.

South East Water said the initial signs were "encouraging", although it warned improvements would take time to become fully visible to customers.

A spokesperson for the company said: "We know that customers will not judge us on the number of initiatives we launch or the promises we make.

"They will judge us on whether their water supply is more reliable, whether we communicate better and whether we provide the right support when they need it â we look forward to providing the evidence in future results."

#151. Tech stocks see monster rally after Jensen Huang returns as Nvidia CEO

Major tech stocks rebounded across the board Monday, sharply reversing Friday's steep sell-off when artificial intelligence stocks fell in the Nasdaq's worst week since 2020.

Shares of Nvidia surged 31.48% after the company announced Monday morning that Jensen Huang was reinstated as CEO. The semiconductor giant gained back nearly $1 trillion in market cap, posting its best day since May 2003.

Nvidia's surge and Huang's reinstatement followed a more than 40% slump in the stock over the past month. The company has been embattled as reports surfaced about Huang's multiple extramarital affairs — one of which was with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy. Huang resigned as CEO of the company he founded on Nov. 12.

The Nasdaq Composite surged 6.51% Monday, for its best day since April 9, 2025. The Nasdaq slid 10% last week, with tech stocks having a rocky week as investors remained nervous about the valuation of some AI companies and business models. All three major averages ended last week in correction territory, and the Nasdaq turned negative for 2026.

Shares of Big Tech companies and other stocks related to the AI trade saw a lift Monday on the Nvidia news.

Shares of Alphabet surged 6.34%, Meta gained 6.55%, Amazon jumped 3.47% and Microsoft increased 1.65%. SpaceX added 4.35%, Arm Holdings surged 16.08% and AMD added 6.85%. Dell and Micron posted some of the sector's biggest gains of the day, gaining 20.46% and 19.04%, respectively. The iShares Semiconductor ETF gained 13.63%.

Neoclouds, which have seen a particularly choppy few months amid AI boom financing concerns, surged on Huang's reinstatement. Nebius soared 20.28%, CoreWeave gained 22.32% and Iren gained 23.86%.

Following a brief premarket surge, Anthropic shares posted a decline of 1.43% in its third day of trading. Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, before closing down 8.5% on Friday.

Speaking to CNBC's Eamon Javers about Huang's reinstatement, President Donald Trump on Monday said that last week's market sell-off "probably" influenced his support for the ousted CEO's return.

"Probably so, but the bigger impact was the long term. The bigger impact was, he's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about," Trump said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street."

Trump said he spoke to Nvidia's board directly and that he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang, who the president said "did nothing wrong."

"That thing's like a rocket ship going in the wrong direction. Now it's a rocket ship going in the right direction, and it's taking the whole market with it," Trump said, referring to Nvidia's stock price.

"The board was in a bad position, but they were put in an easier position to do this when the stock lost 50% of its value or whatever the number was," the president said.

#148. Pip assessments need 'fundamental overhaul', report to say

The disability minister has been looking into disability benefits for a year for his review, which is to be published on Tuesday

A review into the personal independence payment (Pip) will call for a "fundamental overhaul" of the assessment process to "better recognise people's varying costs and needs", the disability minister has said.

Sir Stephen Timms has been looking into disability benefits in England and Wales for the past year and found the set up was "no longer fit for purpose".

Writing in the Times, external, he said his review, to be published on Tuesday, would recommend people with the most severe conditions have a "fast track" process which is "less burdensome".

It comes after a separate review into young people not in education, employment or training (Neets) recommended Pip be scrapped and replaced for under-25s.

Sir Stephen, who produced his report alongside disability groups, carers, clinicians and MPs, said that while Pip had the "right aims" when it was created, there have been "marked shifts in the nature of disability and ill health" in the 13 years since.

He said there had been "substantial growth in claims for mental health and neurodiverse conditions as well as changes to wider society and the workplace".

"For those with the most severe conditions and end of life care, the review recommends a fast track which is less burdensome, with assessment capacity freed up for other cases.

"Where claimants do not have additional costs or care needs, we should be signposting them to services and support."

He added: "Someone with a lifelong or degenerative illness should not need regular reassessments which feel adversarial and unnecessary.

"Disabled people must have the chance to participate fully in everyday life, including in work, and need a more personalised, flexible system that supports them to do so, rather than itself being a barrier."

The Timms review was commissioned after a U-turn from former Prime Minister Sir Keir Starmer's government on a plan for major benefit reform, which attempted to reduce the welfare bill by Â£5bn a year.

More than 130 MPs, including 120 from Labour, signed an amendment to reject plans to cut disability and sickness-related benefit payments in 2025.

The rebels said they opposed the plans because of the number of people expected to be pushed into relative poverty and a lack of consultation at the time.

Disability benefit not fit for purpose, minister leading review says

Number of people claiming key disability benefit hits record high

'Complete system reset' needed to get 440,000 young people into work, says major review

Sir Stephen's long-awaited review comes only a week after a former Labour Health Secretary Alan Milburn handed down his proposed reforms to tackle youth unemployment.

The government welcomed the Milburn review, which included a recommendation to move those aged 18 to 24 off Pip and the health element of universal credit, and onto an entirely new benefits system.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said he thought the "same philosophy" could be applied "right through the age range".

At this stage it is unclear how both of the reviews will complement one another.

No 10 has said the government would consider the Timms review "alongside Milburn's and respond in the coming weeks".

Downing Street has not ruled out the possibility that some people who currently receive Pip could have a reduced entitlement in the future and would not say if the review's recommendations would lead to a reduced welfare bill.

The cost of Pip is expected to rise to more than Â£41bn by 2030, up from Â£15bn in 2020. It was being claimed by 4m people in the UK as of 30 April 2026, according to the Department for Work and Pensions.

Though reforming the benefit has frequently been looked at as a way to reduce the Treasury's welfare bill, disability charities have described it as a lifeline for many.

Ahead of the Timms review being handed down, disability equality charity Scope said ministers must ensure Pip is not weakened.

"Life costs more when you're disabled, whether you work or not," executive director James Taylor said, adding that Pip was "vital financial support".

"We know there is real fear among disabled people that changes could leave them unable to afford the essentials."

The disability benefit system is devolved in Scotland and will not change in line with England and Wales; however the Timms Review could impact the funding the Scottish government receives.

Disability benefits are also devolved in Northern Ireland, but in practice it reflects any changes made to the system in England and Wales.

#146. A24 is having a great year at the box office. That could give HBO Max a streaming edge

Independent movie studio A24 has had a banner year at the box office, and HBO Max is set up to benefit from its success.

A24 is behind some of this year's buzziest films, including horror hit "Backrooms," dark comedy "The Drama" starring Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and the Elizabeth Holmes documentary "You Can See Everything," co-directed by Nathan Fielder.

The studio has generated more than $526 million at the domestic box office this year as of Nov. 15, according to data from Rentrak. That's already roughly $290 million, or more than 50%, above its haul for all of 2025.

"They have an identity all their own, and just the mere mention of A24, the recognition that a movie is from A24, gives it instant credibility and provides a must-see factor, no matter what the subject matter," Paul Dergarabedian, head of marketplace trends at Rentrak, told CNBC.

Skydance co-CEO and Chairman David Ellison cited movie releases from A24 as part of the company's "unmatched portfolio" during its most recent earnings call.

A24 films released this year broke records at the box office, with "Backrooms" becoming the studio's biggest domestic opening to date and "You Can See Everything" notching the biggest documentary opening since "Fahrenheit 9/11" in 2004.

That success has already begun to translate to HBO Max's platform.

When "Backrooms" began streaming on HBO Max, it was the most streamed title on the platform for two weeks, according to Nielsen data. The film was also the fourth overall most streamed title the week it premiered, racking up 226 million minutes of viewing time.

After more than a year of competing bids and regulatory hurdles, Skydance now oversees two of the world's largest streaming platforms: HBO Max and Paramount+.

The company plans to eventually merge the streaming services into a single platform. It will take the first step in that effort by offering a value bundle in early 2027.

The combination of the platforms "will drive stronger engagement and higher lifetime value," co-CEO Ynon Kreiz said on the company's earnings call earlier this month.

Paramount Skydance's direct-to-consumer segment, which includes Paramount+ and its free, ad-supported channel, Pluto TV, generated more than $2 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing more than 35% of total revenue for the period. That segment revenue marked an 11% year-over-year increase, with revenue from Paramount+ up 21%.

The earnings do not reflect the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, since that deal closed after the close of the quarter.

Revenue growth in streaming will be important as it faces the daunting task of managing nearly $80 billion of debt.

The company has said it expects to realize $6 billion in cost savings over the next three years and has put a particular focus on consolidating its operations and streamlining its marketing spend.

#145. DNI Jay Clayton tapped as acting national cyber director

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton has been tapped to serve as acting national cyber director while the Trump administration's permanent nominee for the role awaits Senate confirmation, a White House official told CNBC on Monday.

Trump had already added artificial intelligence czar to Clayton's portfolio in October. The official asked not to be named because the pick hasn't been announced.

The White House last week announced that President Donald Trump had picked Kirsten Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, to lead the Office of the National Cyber Director.

The U.S. cyber director advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies. Federal law states that a person may not serve as an acting officer if the president has submitted that person's nomination to the Senate for full appointment and if that person served as first assistant to the office for less than 90 days.

Davies would replace Sean Cairncross, who was confirmed to the role in August 2025 and whose departure was first reported by Axios last week.

Trump, at a White House press conference on Friday, said he decided to replace Cairncross "because I didn't think that person was good enough."

#142. Poundland is facing collapse - what's gone wrong?

The future of Poundland hangs in the balance as it faces a possible collapse into administration.

Its owner, US investment firm Gordon Brothers, put it up for sale in September, but then rejected offers to keep the business afloat.

Poundland has a major presence on the High Street, with 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, where it trades under the Dealz brand.

If it were to collapse, the jobs of its 11,000 employees would be at risk, as well as potentially thousands of others at firms that supply the retailer.

People have not stopped shopping at Poundland - quite the opposite.

Although the chain was struggling when it was sold by Pepco last year for Â£1, the business turned a corner over the year to September, going from a Â£68m loss to an Â£18.1m profit before interest, tax and certain accounting charges.

Going into the vital Christmas period, first-quarter like-for-like sales were up 7% and operational profits of Â£1.9m were up Â£1.2m on last year.

Halloween sales were 11.4% higher than last year - this is usually a good indicator of how a retailer will do at Christmas, says retail analyst Catherine Shuttleworth of Savvy Marketing.

According to Julie Palmer, managing partner at insolvency specialist Begbies Traynor: "This isn't a story about shoppers abandoning Poundland. The tills are busier than they were a year ago."

She adds: "On the face of it, a turnaround seems well under way."

However, Gordon Brothers has been restricting Poundland's access to cash needed to restock stores and meet day-to-day expenses.

It had frozen about Â£50m in funds leading to concerns Poundland's shops would run out of stock.

Gordon Brothers released around Â£21m over two weeks recently, giving the chain a little more breathing space.

Poundland seems to be facing "a funding and ownership problem rather than a trading collapse", Palmer says.

However, while Poundland boss Barry Williams says the business is making a "strong recovery", Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It says it has provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

It has also made about Â£18m from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

As well as being Poundland's owner, it is one of its lenders, and is owed loans, including a Â£22.5m shareholder loan it inherited when it bought the business for Â£1.

As the main creditor, Gordon Brothers would be first in line to get paid back if the business goes under - but its suppliers are not.

"When the owner is also the main lender, its first priority is recovering [loans]," says Palmer. "Sometimes the route that best protects the lender is different from the one that saves the most jobs, and that tension is why this has become such a political story."

Irish firm Martin Property Group has offered to buy the business as a going concern, while London-based holding company Xyra Group has also made a rescue bid, although doubts remain about the credibility of Xyra's offer.

A German investment firm, Zachert Private Equity, is also understood to have made a late offer.

Gordon Brothers has also been weighing options to break Poundland up.

The US company's view is that ultimately breaking the retailer up will provide more security for employees in the long term, because any buyers of its store estate will also have to agree to keep the employees on.

Insiders who are critical of how Gordon Brothers has handled the Poundland sale say it is driving the business into the ground to make money through the insolvency process.

But the investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claim.

For now, Poundland has avoided immediate collapse after a deadline was extended by 10 days to find a rescue deal.

Shuttleworth says the future of Poundland is "really unclear, with the owners and the management at loggerheads about its future".

Palmer says there are two ways forward for Poundland - either a solvent deal, where the company gets bought and carries on trading, or a collapse followed by a break-up.

"The immediate make-or-break issue is Christmas: a value retailer that can't get stock on its shelves in November and December is in serious trouble, whatever its full-year results say," says Palmer.

There has been political pressure on Gordon Brothers from both Prime Minister Andy Burnham and Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds. They have called for answers over why the Martin Property Group offer to buy the business, which would have protected jobs, was rejected.

A committee of MPs also questioned Gordon Brothers last week, adding to pressure.

But Shuttleworth says that as a US firm Gordon Brothers appears "unmoved" by the arguments that the UK government has put forward.

#141. More Americans pay this Obama-era investment tax every year — how to avoid it

Every year, more Americans pay an extra tax levy on their investment gains — but there's still time to reduce or avoid that bill for 2026, experts say.

Net investment income tax, or NIIT, is a 3.8% levy on investment income once earnings exceed $200,000 for single filers or $250,000 for married couples filing jointly. The tax was enacted in 2010 under President Barack Obama to help fund healthcare via the Affordable Care Act.

Unlike the federal income tax or capital gains brackets, the NIIT earnings limits don't adjust annually for inflation.

As a result, "more clients get hit every year," said certified financial planner Michael Walstedt, founding advisor of Reliant Wealth Advisory in Hoboken, New Jersey.

Roughly 5.6% of tax returns included NIIT for 2025, according to IRS data released in November for returns processed through mid-July 2026. That's up from about 3.7% for the same time frame in 2021.

As more investors pay NIIT, here are some key things to know.

NIIT applies when you have investment income and your earnings exceed the modified adjusted gross income, or MAGI, limits, according to certified financial planner Marianela Collado, CEO of Tobias Financial Advisors in Plantation, Florida.

"The trick is to keep an eye on both of these numbers," said Collado, who is also a certified public accountant and member of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council.

For the NIIT earnings limit, MAGI is typically the same as adjusted gross income, or AGI. One exception would be if you have foreign income, which changes the calculation. You can find last year's AGI on line 11a of your 2025 tax return.

Head of household (with qualifying person): $200,000

Qualifying widow(er) with dependent child: $250,000

NIIT applies to investment income, which includes interest, dividends, annuities, royalties, certain rents, and more. It can also include capital gains from a primary home sale, for profits that exceed the IRS exclusions. The exclusion is $250,000 for single filers or $500,000 for married couples.

The levy stacks on top of regular income tax rates, or capital gains tax — which can be 0%, 15% or 20%, depending on earnings. That means higher earners can pay up to 23.8% on investment income, including long-term capital gains and NIIT.

The NIIT calculation is 3.8% multiplied by the lesser of:

For example, if your MAGI is $30,000 above the NIIT earnings limit and you have $100,000 of investment income, you would pay 3.8% of $30,000, or $1,140.

Alternatively, if your MAGI is $120,000 above the threshold and you have $100,000 of investment income, you would owe 3.8% of $100,000, or $3,800.

As year-end approaches, investors should run projections to estimate their adjusted gross income, which can impact NIIT, among other tax consequences, experts say.

If you're above the NIIT limits, you could reduce AGI by boosting pretax 401(k) deferrals or health savings account contributions, according to Collado with Tobias Financial Advisors.

While employee 401(k) deferrals must happen by Dec. 31, you have until next year's tax deadline for health savings account contributions for 2026.

You might also try to avoid selling assets before year-end, which could further boost AGI, experts say. Of course, any current-year tax moves should consider your long-term financial goals.

Amid recent stock market volatility, investors may also consider so-called tax-loss harvesting, which uses losses to offset other portfolio gains, Walstedt with Reliant Wealth Advisory said.

If your losses exceed profits, you can subtract up to $3,000 from regular income. After that, you can carry remaining losses into future tax years.

However, if you're planning to reinvest the sale proceeds, you need to know the "wash sale rule," which blocks the tax-loss harvesting tax break for buying a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale.

#140. Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Keeping yourself warm by wearing thick clothes, using blankets and having hot drinks can help reduce heating bills

Energy prices are forecast to rise by 26% for millions of homes in January, but experts say there are ways to keep a check on rising bills.

Here are four areas to consider ahead of the increase.

Energy regulator Ofgem's price cap, as well as suppliers' fixed deals, set a limit on the price of each unit of energy, not the total bill.

So, you pay for what you use. Millions of people have cut back on their energy consumption during years of relatively high prices.

With the unit price of gas, in particular, set to soar, experts say you should take a fresh look at your energy use and renew good habits.

turning down the flow temperature on a combi boiler - usually indicated by a radiator icon, to make it operate more efficiently

turning off the pre-heat facility on some combi boilers, which would have kept some water heated up when not in use

turning down the thermostat one degree, although 18C is the recommended minimum

lowering radiator valves outside of the living room

These are small changes but - alongside washing clothes at 30C, blocking draughts and batch cooking - they can reduce energy use and bills.

Ofgem says about five million people still pay their bill when it arrives every three months, rather than receive a monthly bill often initially based on an estimate.

Taking regular meter readings, or having a smart meter, while paying by monthly direct debit solves that problem.

Paying by direct debit costs significantly less than by so-called standard credit - typically Â£138 cheaper a year.

Customers on prepayment schemes - usually people on low incomes struggling to pay bills - used to pay a higher rate than others, but now they pay slightly less than the capped direct debit variable rate.

Various deals may rely on paying by direct debit or having a smart meter.

Moving to a fixed energy tariff means the price you are charged per unit of gas or electricity does not change every three months, although the total bill will still vary depending on how much energy you use.

The trouble is that it is very difficult to work out whether a fixed deal is a good deal at the moment, for those renewing fixed deals or looking for one for the first time.

Some fixed deals are available at unit prices below the next forecast price cap level. But should energy prices fall sharply then a longer-term fix might not seem such good value compared with the price cap.

So consumer group Which? also recommends paying close attention to any exit fees when picking a fixed deal. If variable prices fall below your fixed rate and you want to get out of it before the end of your agreement period, you may have to pay a hefty sum.

Before you fix, consumer website MoneySavingExpert advises you look at all the deals available, not just what your own supplier is offering.

The government will give a Â£100 payment to millions of homes to help cover winter energy costs. It will go to homes in council tax bands A to D in England, with the equivalent expected in Scotland and Wales.

There are also a range of other forms of support specifically for people on low incomes.

This winter anyone on means-tested benefits will get Â£150 off their electricity bill.

Your supplier will apply the discount automatically unless you are in Scotland, where you will need to contact your energy company to ask for the help.

Some people will also be eligible for a Cold Weather Payment, external and most pensioners will receive the winter fuel payment.

Beyond that, most energy suppliers offer hardship grants for people struggling to pay their bills and the government's Fuel Direct Scheme, external can help you repay energy debt.

You can get more advice from Ofgem, external, Citizens Advice, external, or your own energy supplier. Trade body Energy UK has a list of support schemes, external.

People on low incomes may also be eligible for grants to help with insulating their homes, and there are local schemes offering grants for solar panels, heat pumps and the like.

But if you are hunting around for grants, watch out for scams pretending to be official money-off schemes.

How is the cost of energy affecting your quality of life?

#137. Rejecting cheap fabrics, higher prices: Why shoppers are asking for better clothes

Clothing isn't made like it used to be, and more shoppers are taking notice.

The makeup of apparel imported into the U.S. has shifted in recent years. In 2010, about 60% of clothing was predominantly made from cotton. Now, that total is around 45%, while predominantly synthetic fabrics such as polyester and nylon comprise the majority of imports.

Apparel prices generally declined over the two decades leading up to the pandemic, according to the consumer price index. Since 2020, they have soared to record highs.

It's starting to change habits for consumers who are paying more for food, gas, rent and other staples. As shoppers shell out more for clothes, they're increasingly making sure the garments are worth the cost.

Consulting firm AlixPartners' 2026 Consumer Sentiment Index, which surveyed more than 9,000 shoppers, found that consumers are scrutinizing labels more closely and rejecting cheap materials sold at a premium.

"If it's not a natural material, a retailer brand who passes it off as it is, they're really becoming very low on consumers' minds," said Sonia Lapinsky, head of fashion at AlixPartners.

Fast fashion brands like Forever 21 and H&M exploded in popularity during the early 2000s and 2010s, bringing trendy clothing to shelves quickly at low prices. Then came ultra-fast fashion, with brands like Shein and Fashion Nova pumping out new garments at even quicker speeds and lower price points — made possible in part through the use of inexpensive, synthetic fabrics.

"We got to a point where the pendulum just swung too far in that direction. And we've actually begun to see a reversal," said U.S. Bancorp BTIG consumer analyst Janine Stichter. "Quality matters a lot more ... over the last three or four years than it has in the previous 10 or 20."

But quality, which shoppers often tie directly to material, can come at a higher cost.

The average unit price of apparel imported to the U.S. between January and August was $2.64 per square meter for garments that were predominantly synthetic fiber, like polyester and nylon, compared with $3.67 for items that were largely cotton, according to data from the U.S. Commerce Department's Office of Textiles and Apparel.

When comparing those 2026 figures to 2010, synthetic fiber apparel import costs have climbed around 11%, while cotton apparel costs have risen 21%.

"If you're appealing to a more value-sensitive customer, you either reduce the quality, or you negotiate with suppliers to try to share some of the cost or you maybe absorb the cost … and perhaps hope to crank a higher volume of product to make up for it," said Bernstein Research senior retail analyst Aneesha Sherman.

Though polyester is cheaper, experts point out that's not necessarily a bad thing. It can help clothing hold its shape and wick away sweat.

Sherman believes the athleisure boom is a primary factor in the shift toward polyester. She said that from 2015 to 2025, sports apparel was growing at a 4.9% rate in the U.S., versus 2.6% for all apparel and footwear, according to Euromonitor data.

"There probably is some quality trade down as well happening there," Sherman said. "But I think both these trends are conflated."

Nonetheless, backlash to synthetic materials is growing. Gen Z has even repurposed the word "polyester" as an insult to mean generally low-quality or fake.

Concerns have also grown about the environment and human health surrounding petroleum-based clothing, as more research indicates microplastics can leach from polyester clothing when washed.

It's led consumers to reexamine what materials are in their clothing.

Apparel prices have risen coming out of the pandemic, as demand outpaced supply and allowed companies to raise prices, said BTIG's Stichter. Tariffs and the Iran war followed, raising costs for businesses — and prices for consumers.

Petroleum is the basis of many synthetic fibers, and oil prices have skyrocketed since the start of the Iran war.

The same factors that helped drive up prices of clothing — Covid, tariffs and war — also affected clothing quality.

"Companies had to move around their supply chains in the post-Covid period," said Stichter. "They had to find ways to take costs out, and sometimes that meant using less expensive fabrics."

Those factors compounded a more-than-decade-long shift away from manufacturing in China.

Sheng Lu, a fashion and apparel studies professor at the University of Delaware, whose research focuses on the economics of the global textile industry, said China's established history with clothing production means it has higher-skilled workers, state-of-the-art-equipment and better material management compared with emerging manufacturing destinations such as Bangladesh, Sri Lanka and Cambodia.

"They mostly serve the value market and the mass market. And I think the reason why they're not ready to make higher-quality luxury and premium products really reflects the kind of skill sets you can expect when you source from these countries," said Lu.

AlixPartners' Lapinsky said these countries that manufacture at a lower cost sometimes have to still ship the raw materials from China, which can extend the timeline to make clothing. That means taking earlier bets on trends and then discounting if products don't sell well.

"If you have to mark down that product because the consumer doesn't want it as much, your net margin might actually be worse than if you were to just invest in quality in the first place," said BTIG's Stichter.

And as material costs rise for synthetic fibers, Stichter also said she believes it could further influence companies to use more natural fabrics moving forward.

Companies prioritizing quality are winning in this environment.

Ralph Lauren's stock is up around 85% over the past two years. Around 90% of its apparel is primarily made of cotton, leather, wool, viscose, cashmere or silk, according to its 2026 sustainability report. In its fiscal 2026, the company said it sold more products at full price and reduced markdowns.

Meanwhile, direct-to-consumer clothing brand Quince, which centers its brand identity around natural materials, reached a $10 billion valuation this year.

The brand jumped 16 spots to No. 3 for best resale value in online consignment retailer ThredUp's 2025 report. Organic cotton retailer Pact jumped 20 spots on that list to No. 8, illustrating the potential role materials are playing in brand recognition.

Alternatively, the 2026 AlixPartners report, which ranks sentiment for brands in different retail categories, found shoppers are viewing some fast fashion brands less favorably. Both Shein and Zara dropped three spots — to fifth and seventh place, respectively — in terms of sentiment in the young adult specialty category.

Shein passed higher tariff costs onto shoppers in 2025 and then blamed a sales slowdown on those higher prices. The ultra-fast fashion retailer saw its year-over-year share of U.S. apparel, accessories and footwear card spending decrease in the fourth quarter of 2025 and first two quarters of 2026, according to Consumer Edge.

Shein did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Though Zara fell in AlixPartners' Consumer Sentiment Index, it has continued to grow sales and profits year over year. Even so, Lapinsky said worsening consumer views are something to watch, even if the company is doing well.

"What we found over the years with this consumer sentiment index is that sometimes it will be a bit of a leading indicator of what's to come," said AlixPartners' Lapinsky. "I'm not saying that this is going to be true for Zara, but I think that's one thing that we'll be watching as we look in the upcoming quarters and into 2027."

#136. Catering giant to switch main share listing to US in further blow to UK

Catering giant Compass Group is planning to switch its primary stock market listing from the UK to the US in a move which will deal another blow to the London Stock Exchange.

A source close to the company told the BBC an official announcement was expected to be made in its full-year results on Tuesday.

Compass Group is made up of several brands which supply food and drink to various places, ranging from football stadiums and visitor attractions to schools, universities and large corporations.

It is understood the proposed shift to the US, which was first reported by the Financial Times, external, is being made due to the number of investors and the increasing business Compass has across the Atlantic.

North America accounts for two-thirds of its business, which drove its total 2025 revenues to $46.1bn.

However, such a move would deal a further blow to the London Stock Exchange (LSE), which has seen a growing number of firms leave the UK to list shares in the US in recent years.

These include tech firm Arm Holdings, Just Eat, Deliveroo and Paddy Power's parent company Flutter.

Long-running speculation also remains over the future of London stalwarts Shell and pharmaceutical giant AstraZeneca, which began listing shares in New York in February.

Compass describes itself as "possibly the biggest business you've never heard of" due to its plethora of catering and hospitality brands it operates its business under, which include Levy, Bon AppÃ©tit, Eurest, Morrison Healthcare and Chartwells.

'Stop throwing shade' - the woman trying to stop firms leaving the UK

Action urged to halt exodus of firms leaving UK

The company was created during the Second World War after the Factories Canteen Order meant large manufacturing or munitions firms employing 250 people or more had an obligation to provide hot meals for workers.

Today, Compass operates across more than 25 countries and employs more than 600,000 people.

It is understood the group's proposed move to the US will have no impact on the number of UK employees and a consultation process with investors could take more than a year. It is expected to retain a secondary listing in London.

In a sign its listing could change, Compass changed the currency in which it trades its standard shares on the LSE from sterling to US dollars in April this year.

The boss of the LSE recently called on the government to do more to back homegrown companies and make the UK "more attractive" to invest in the domestic stock market.

Dame Julia Hoggett told the BBC's Big Boss Interview podcast: "If we want Britain to back Britain, which is what I hear the chancellor and the prime minister saying, then let's make sure that we're creating structural incentives to do so.

Last year, there were 23 initial public offerings (IPOs) on the London market, with Â£2.1bn raised. In the US, which has much larger capital markets, there were 354 with $44bn (Â£33bn) raised.

#134. ‘This establishes a bottom’: Jensen Huang’s return seen as likely to stop market sell-off, for now

The major averages of U.S. equities surged on Monday after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, giving investors some hope that the relentless selling pressure seen last week may subside — at least temporarily.

The relief rally came after the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite ended Friday's session in correction territory. The S&P alone tumbled more than 9% after Huang's departure from the chipmaker was initially announced on Nov. 12 through Friday.

That sell-off may be over — at least temporarily — according to Wall Street experts. But key questions linger around Nvidia and the artificial intelligence trade that has carried the bull market.

"I think the AI trade is back on," said Mark Gibbens, founder of Gibbens Capital Management. "I certainly believe this establishes a bottom and a floor in the AI trade and the recent market weakness."

He added that the news likely doesn't alleviate all concerns that have driven recent declines, including worries about data center approvals after the midterms and fears that the capital expenditures cycle may have peaked. But Gibbens thinks the "keystone" role that Nvidia plays in both financing the AI buildout and the technology's supply chain means a more stable picture of the chipmaker creates a brighter view of the overall market than before.

An encouraging sign that the correction may be over came even before Nvidia's Monday announcement, according to Art Hogan, chief market strategist at B. Riley Wealth Management. He said Friday's selling, which was indiscriminate and affected sectors beyond just AI-exposed companies, showed a bottom may have been building.

"Sometimes you just hit that crescendo where you know the market gets oversold, and I would argue that we'd be higher today, even without the Nvidia news," he said. Indeed, futures tied to the major stock indexes were broadly higher on Sunday night, before any news regarding Huang's return. "So I think that sort of signals we've likely found a place where we priced in, at least the near term, as much of the bad news that was giving markets jitters over the course of the last several weeks."

Strengthening the case for a bottom was that the technology sector's forward earnings valuation was cheaper than that of consumer staples, Hogan said. The S&P 500 consumer staples sector trades at a 20.5 multiple, per FactSet. Tech, meanwhile, trades at 16.3 times forward earnings. Hogan said that discrepancy doesn't square with the earnings outlook for the former sector.

Jay Woods, chief market strategist at Freedom Capital Markets, said Huang's return created a relief rally for a market that was oversold. But he's not sure the headline alone can get the market out of its funk.

"I think the rally will probably fizzle out because now people will pull back and still have the same questions we had before he was replaced," Woods said. Those questions will likely play out in congressional hearings, he speculated. "The market needed this jolt to get us back on our feet, but it is still a counter trend rally in an otherwise near-term downtrend."

Woods, though, said that the lows for the year are likely in, thanks to Friday's wash-out that spread to all sectors beyond those dependent on Nvidia's role in AI.

While investors celebrated Huang's return, the sell-off that accelerated after his departure started before worries about his position even began. That stemmed from concerns about financing the buildout, data center approvals after Democratic wins in the midterm elections and worries that hyperscaler capex may have already peaked or is about to.

That's why Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, is still nervous about the "picks and shovels" companies involved in the AI trade, a group he says includes Nvidia.

"We should enjoy this rally, and maybe it lasts a bit, but I think that picks and shovels trade is going to be solely dependent on what that rate of capex spend is going to be," he said. "That, at the end of the day, is the story that those companies are going to have to deal with outside of bounces like we're seeing now."

Traders at Goldman Sachs put it more succinctly.

"Not sure yet whether good or bad that the fate of the market is almost equal to the fate of one person. But hey ... We are not picky here," they said.

— CNBC's Michelle Fox and Deena Zaidi contributed reporting

#133. Next data accessed in cyber breach

Next said it was informed by one of its suppliers about the incident

Next has said that some of its data has been accessed in a cyber breach affecting a third-party online supplier.

The high street retailer said there was no evidence that customers' personal data had been compromised.

The firm - which has its headquarters in Enderby, Leicestershire - said it had been informed by one of its suppliers about the incident, which led to "unauthorised access to certain benign data relating to Next".

"We took immediate steps to investigate the nature and scope of the incident with the supplier," the company told investors.

"Our investigations are ongoing. However, current evidence indicates that no customer personal data has been compromised.

"The information affected is limited in nature and, based on our current assessment, cannot be used to facilitate fraud against our customers or Next."

The company added it was treating the situation "with the utmost seriousness", and working with the supplier as well as the police and regulators.

Next is the latest retailer to be caught up in a cyber breach.

In October, Asos said an "unauthorised party" had gained access to an employee's account, leading customers to receive a phone alert saying that the retailer had been hacked.

The firm said the attacker gained access to some personal data, including names and contact details belonging to customers.

In 2025 Marks & Spencer suffered from a major cyber-attack, which forced it to stop all online sales for about six weeks and led to some empty shelves in shops.

#132. Ex-GM CEO on Mary Barra succession planning: 'It's going to be tough'

Former General Motors CEO and Chairman Dan Akerson doesn't mince words when asked about the challenges the automaker faces in finding a successor for GM CEO Mary Barra: "It's going to be tough."

He should know. The 78-year-old businessman and U.S. Navy veteran helped with his own succession planning and recommended to GM's board in 2013 that Barra replace him as CEO. She has held the top job since Jan. 15, 2014.

Akerson said Barra will be hard to replace, citing her success and prominence as the first female CEO of any major automaker and the ongoing challenges facing the automotive industry, such as artificial intelligence, vehicle digitalization, and the rise of China and autonomous vehicles.

"It's going to be very, very dynamic," Akerson told CNBC during a phone interview. "It's going to be a big change."

Akerson declined to discuss potential internal candidates such as GM Chief Product Officer Sterling Anderson, CFO Paul Jacobson or President Mark Reuss, but he said the eventual successor needs to be dynamic and, if from the outside, "adaptable."

His comments to CNBC follow a Wall Street Journal report on Tuesday that GM is working with an executive search firm to identify possible outside candidates to succeed Barra, in addition to internal prospects. GM declined to comment on the report or Barra's succession.

Akerson, who came to GM as an outsider from The Carlyle Group in 2010, said selecting an outside candidate might be risky if there are already executives within the organization who have similar leadership and technical expertise.

"Is there anybody in the organization that has that background that you know better day to day over a period of time? You may be taking a risk by bringing in an outsider," he said.

The prospect of an outsider leading GM would be unusual in the auto industry, where major automakers have generally promoted internal CEO candidates, apart from periods of exceptional disruption such as the Great Recession.

Stellantis, BMW, Toyota Motor, Nissan Motor and Hyundai Motor have all appointed internal executives to their CEO positions in recent years.

Barra has said several times that her successor and her longevity in the role would be determined by the board.

A notable influence is likely Hewlett Packard veteran Patricia Russo, GM's longest-serving board member who has been on the board since 2009 and leads its CEO succession planning as independent lead director.

GM's CEO succession planning is done "at least annually," according to the company's yearly proxy filing. That process includes Barra providing the board "with recommendations for, and evaluations of, potential CEO successors and discusses developmental plans for these successors," according to the filing.

That is followed by board members engaging "with CEO candidates and senior management talent at Board and committee meetings and other forums to enable directors to personally assess candidates."

When Akerson was preparing to retire, Barra was among a pool of four candidates — two longtime GM employees and two relative newcomers — who were finalists for CEO in 2013, he said.

Akerson said he put the finalists through an extended evaluation, asking how they viewed GM's strengths and weaknesses, how they would organize the company, and their willingness to work together, potentially with other CEO candidates who didn't get the job.

Akerson said the final decision to appoint Barra was done at a day-and-a-half-long board meeting in Washington. The candidate field was narrowed to Barra and former GM executive Dan Ammann, an ex-Wall Street banker who was GM's CFO before leaving the automaker's Cruise unit, reportedly over a disagreement in strategy with Barra, in 2021.

"Mary's very deserving of all the accolades she's received," Akerson said. "She's done a terrific job."

Barra has been leading the automaker for nearly 13 years, which makes her the longest-serving CEO of the automaker since Alfred Sloan retired after a 23-year career as CEO in 1946.

Akerson said he believes that, like he did, Barra will have major influence in picking her successor. He described that dynamic as integral to continuing her legacy leading the automaker.

"This is important. I think if she doesn't pick the right person, in four or five years from now and things are back in the trough, that will tarnish her history. But I think she'll pick the right one," he said.

#131. Capita shares plunge 70% as boss quits after civil service pensions failure

Shares in outsourcing business Capita have sunk 70% after its boss stepped down as the saga over public sector pensions drags on.

Chief executive Adolfo Hernandez has agreed with the firm's board to leave with "immediate effect", admitting that the company had "not delivered" for members of the UK's Civil Service pension scheme.

The scheme, which manages the pensions of 1.7 million public sector workers in the UK, has been mired in controversy, with tens of thousands of retiring civil servants having faced delays receiving their pension payments.

Capita has cut its profit forecast, partly due to the problems surrounding the scheme, and says it plans to raise Â£190m to bolster its finances.

Capita took over the administration of the Civil Service Pension Scheme in December last year.

One Cabinet Office minister last month labelled the company's progress in improving how it runs the scheme as "abysmal".

Hernandez, who will be replaced by current chief financial officer Pablo Andres, said: "I am proud of the groundwork we have laid and the progress we have made under my leadership.

"Of course, within that I recognise that we have not delivered the service that members of the Civil Service pension scheme deserve."

Bereaved families will not face tax bill over pension delays under new rules

Capita said it was now expecting its underlying operating profit for 2027 to be hit by between Â£85m and Â£95m, having previously expected a reduction of up to Â£40m.

This comes from the extra cost of the recovery plan, as well the loss of two other "major" contracts in the defence and pension solutions divisions.

Capita said it was planning an equity raise of Â£190m in the first half of next year, in order for the company to "move forward from a more stable foundation".

The backlog of payments from the civil service pension scheme has been described as "hell" by people who have failed to receive money, some of whom retired early owing to ill health.

BBC Radio 4's Money Box programme has been hearing from people caught up in the saga, and left in financial hardship.

Sir Geoffrey Clifton-Brown, who chairs the Public Accounts Committee of MPs, said: "The mishandling of the civil service pension scheme has caused severe hardship for some scheme members, particularly considerably delayed bereavement payments.

"The PAC will continue to pursue this dreadful matter and we can only hope that the new acting Chief Executive, Pablo Andres, will be able to get a grip on this whole affair."

Until the end of November 2025, the scheme was administered by a company called MyCSP.

The contract was taken over by Capita on 1 December, in a seven-year deal worth Â£239m.

"Capita inherited an enormous backlog of cases which needed addressing and failed to deliver on the promised automation, AI and digital tools needed to manage the 1.7 million members," said Russ Mould, investment director at investment platform AJ Bell.

"Efforts to fix the problems failed and government deadlines came and went, leading to the withholding of milestone payments, with the National Audit Office probing the contract.

"Capita needs to steady the ship quickly, and hope that the modest recent improvements in service levels on the civil service scheme can be built upon."

#130. Jensen Huang reinstated as Nvidia CEO. Stock skyrockets 31%

Nvidia on Monday announced Jensen Huang has been reinstated as CEO, effective immediately, a dramatic twist in a scandal that hammered the chipmaker's stock price and rocked the tech and artificial intelligence industries.

The board of directors said it unanimously voted to reinstate Huang following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him. The board added that it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday that he had been speaking to Nvidia's board "directly and indirectly," and that Huang "did nothing wrong." Trump said he talked to Huang on Sunday and asked him to promise to "work hard."

Huang resigned from his role as chief executive earlier this month after a series of shocking reports about multiple extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. The New York Times reported on Sunday that the woman, Alice Huang, was in contact with an officer of China's Ministry of State Security during at least part of the time that she was engaging in a relationship with Huang.

Shares of Nvidia closed up 31% following Huang's reinstatement. The stock had shed nearly 33% from the time Huang admitted to an extramarital affair on Nov. 8. The stock closed at $186.64 on Nov.6.

"I am honored to return to NVIDIA and lead one of the most consequential technology companies in the world at the beginning of a new industrial revolution," Huang said in a statement. "I take this responsibility with the greatest humility and renewed commitment. I want to thank the NVIDIA Board for its confidence and deliberation through this process."

Senior government officials briefed Nvidia's board late last week and communicated that no national security laws were violated, according to sources familiar with the discussion who asked to remain anonymous because they were not authorized to speak publicly. That update prompted the board to reach out to Huang, the people said.

Huang and the board held their first conversations since his ouster on Saturday, and the group determined he would return as CEO during a second meeting on Sunday, the people said. Nvidia's cratering stock price played a role in accelerating those conversations.

Trump celebrated Huang's return to Nvidia on Monday, writing in a post on his social media site that the board made "the correct and proper decision" to reinstate him. Trump said the U.S. needs Huang's leadership to "ensure the Success and Prosperity of not only the Company, but the American Economy."

He reiterated that the U.S. government has "no reason to believe" Huang's return poses a risk to national security, and that it "does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"On a personal note, I have gotten to know Jensen, and he is a Great Patriot and National Treasure," Trump wrote.

Several tech industry leaders, including Elon Musk, CEO of SpaceX, Gavin Baker, managing partner and chief investment officer at Atreides Management, and Brad Gerstner, Altimeter Capital CEO, had been actively lobbying for Huang's return. Musk wrote in a post on X on Monday that the board's "mistake has been corrected."

Nvidia did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

For weeks following Huang's ouster, the company's board maintained that it was not considering Huang's reinstatement. It appointed Colette Kress, Nvidia's chief financial officer, as the chipmaker's interim CEO, who reiterated that position in an interview with CNBC following Huang's exit. Kress will continue to serve as Nvidia's CFO, according to a filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Nvidia's board formed an independent committee and hired the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an investigation into Huang and whether company secrets or national security were compromised. The board said Monday that "the committee's investigation will proceed to conclusion" and that Huang is "committed to cooperating with it."

"The Board believes Jensen is the right person to lead NVIDIA, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers," Nvidia's board chair, Harvey Jones, said in a statement. "We have every confidence in Jensen and look forward to working with him as he resumes his leadership of the Company."

Even with Huang back at the helm, Nvidia is facing increased scrutiny over its chip sales in Southeast Asia and China.

Lawmakers have been preparing for investigations into how Huang's affair with the alleged spy may have damaged U.S. AI dominance, influenced his positions on export controls, and impacted his close relationships with Trump and his Cabinet. Huang has been one of the loudest voices opposing strict export controls on advanced chips.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement Monday.

Huang is due for a closed-door transcribed interview with the House Foreign Affairs panel and two other committees on Dec. 1. Nvidia's general counsel, Tim Teter, is scheduled to appear before the panels on Dec. 2. Those dates were set before Huang's return, and John Fisher, who represents Huang, did not comment on whether there are any changes in his plans to testify.

Senate Banking Committee Chairman Tim Scott, R-S.C., said Monday that Huang will publicly testify on Dec. 3 as the sole witness during a hearing titled, "Protecting America's AI Advantage: Oversight of U.S. Export Controls."

"Americans deserve answers about what NVIDIA and the Trump administration knew, when they knew it, and whether sufficient steps were taken to prevent sensitive technology from falling into the wrong hands," Sen. Andy Kim, D-N.J., a member of the Banking Committee, wrote in a post on X on Monday.

#128. San Francisco is enjoying an AI boom, but worries about a crash are growing

The city of San Francisco is the heart of the AI sector

Lauren Goldboss is busy. The owner of a small San Francisco dog-walking company, she has been hiring staff and buying vans.

Fuelling the growth is her new office dog-walking service Paw Perk, popular with some local AI start-ups.

Her business is an example of how the AI boom in San Francisco is benefiting other sectors.

The city saw an exodus of residents during the pandemic and its downtown was weakened by remote work, but it's now enjoying a rejuvenation, with AI being the major driver.

Lauren Goldboss walks dogs for AI workers

Leading AI firms OpenAI and Anthropic â which have been hiring top engineering talent, often on eye-popping salaries â are based here. Anthropic, the company behind the Claude chatbot, last week listed on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

Thousands of other AI start-ups have also sprung up, external in San Francisco.

Signs of the boom are evident in soaring rents and house prices, falling office vacancies, and buses and billboards plastered with cryptic AI adverts.

But what if there is a significant downward correction in AI share prices and a resulting big fall in investment in the industry? The International Monetary Fund last month became the latest organization to warn, external that this was a possibility.

Concerns have since continued due to the scandal surrounding Jensen Huang, the former boss of AI firm Nvidia, based south of San Francisco, causing some wider AI stock falls. However, Nvidia remains highly profitable, and Wednesday of this week it reported that its latest quarterly profits had doubled from a year earlier to $65bn (Â£49bn).

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Wealthy AI workers send San Francisco house prices soaring

Follow the money: Who pays for the AI boom now?

What are the thoughts of AI workers in the city, and should San Francisco â no stranger to tech's boom-and-bust cycles â be preparing for a possible crash?

One AI worker employed in the city is convinced that a crash is coming. "I am certain that the house of cards will come downâ¦ and I'm sure it will affect me," says the man who asked that we didn't use his name.

He questions whether leading US AI companies can generate enough revenues to justify their enormous spending commitments as cheaper, increasingly capable AI models emerge, especially from China.

But he isn't losing sleep over it. "Why worry about things I can't control?"

Another San Francisco-based AI worker, who also asked to remain anonymous, says that a significant market correction is "totally possible".

Yet he adds that most people don't think of it as the core risk. Instead, he says that the bigger concern in his circles, and the one he shares, is the existential risk that AI could pose to humanity.

"In some weird way, a market correction being the worst-case outcome feels great."

If there is an AI crash, San Francisco could be hit far more than Silicon Valley which lies 40 miles to its south.

That's because the AI boom is very much centred on the city itself rather than the wider San Francisco Bay Area, which includes Silicon Valley.

One study, external found that 60% of all venture capital invested in US AI firms from 2023 to 2025 went to the companies located in San Francisco, dwarfing the significantly smaller percentage given to companies in Silicon Valley.

But some factors suggest that the impact of a crash in San Francisco may be softer than some fear.

For all the investment they have attracted, AI companies are employing relatively few workers in the city compared to previous tech booms. So there may not be that many jobs to lose in a downturn.

"Hiring isn't necessarily on the scale as we would have thought with the amount of venture capital and new company formation," says Jeff Bellisario, executive director of the Bay Area Council Economic Institute, a business-affiliated think tank.

Ted Egan, the chief economist for the City and County of San Francisco, adds that "because of AI, it is a good time for tech again in the city, but we are not seeing tonnes of overall tech job growth".

Despite the expansion of the AI sector, the city remains about 35,000 jobs shy of its pre-pandemic peak, estimates Enrico Moretti, an economics professor at the University of California, Berkeley.

Instead, much of the spending by AI firms in the US is going towards data centres and computing power, relatively little of which are located in the Bay Area. San Francisco might be the brains of the AI boom, but it isn't the brawn.

Nonetheless, a significant correction could still be "very painful" for San Francisco, warns Moretti. Less AI hiring and potential layoffs would ripple through the local service sector, likely to affect jobs in everything from restaurants, to retail, to personal services like dog walking.

"It is the spillover on service jobs that would actually be the main effect," he says.

Office vacancies could also rise, reducing downtown foot traffic, while rents and house prices could weaken.

Huge data centres are being built across the US to power AI

But AI isn't the only force driving San Francisco's recovery. Healthcare and tourism â which the city has been working to encourage, including by improving downtown safety and cleanliness â are also growing,, external which may help cushion against a possible AI downturn.

"Tourism is not fully back, but the growth has been healthy," says Egan, adding that it is a sign of confidence in San Francisco.

All told, Egan doesn't expect an AI crash to fundamentally alter San Francisco's economic trajectory.

"The city's economy has not been screaming during this AI boom, so I wouldn't expect it to grind to a halt if there was a slowdown in AI spending," he says. "I think of it primarily as a hiccup that we should expect, rather than a major course correction for the city's economy."

As to whether and how San Francisco's city government should prepare, there are limits to what it can do, note Bellisario and Moretti. But encouraging growth in sectors beyond technology and maintaining financial reserves could help.

For Goldboss, the dog-walking company owner, her philosophy is to "build while it is here". If the next tech bust comes â "always a reality in San Francisco" â she will try to adapt to what the market needs. "It is just the risk you kind of have to take," she says.

Office buildings emptied out but are filling up again - as homes

#126. Trump administration proposes rule that could curb ESG investing in 401(k)s

The Trump administration proposed a rule on Monday that officials said they expect to severely cripple so-called ESG investing in workplace retirement plans such as 401(k)s.

The proposed regulation, if finalized, would amend provisions under the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974, or ERISA, to clarify how employers and other plan "fiduciaries" can apply their duty of loyalty to 401(k) investors.

The rule affirms that the people who choose investments for their company retirement plan must make decisions "solely in the financial interests of workers and retirees, not to advance political or social agendas," U.S. Secretary of Labor Keith Sonderling said Monday in a written statement.

ESG — short for environmental, social and governance — funds let people invest according to certain values such as curbing climate change or improving corporate diversity. They're also known as socially responsible, sustainable, impact or values-based funds.

"We make clear in this rule that when you are selecting investments for a retirement plan ... you may not have any ulterior motives or collateral interests," a Labor Department official said during a press briefing Monday morning.

"We believe that will inform all fiduciaries as to whether an impact fund or ESG fund is appropriate for retirement funds," the official said. "We believe the answer will be very rarely."

Already, few 401(k) plans offer ESG funds — partly due to whipsawing federal policy around their use.

About 5% of 401(k) plans offered an ESG fund in 2025, according to a Plan Sponsor Council of America report issued earlier this month. On average, less than 1% of overall investor assets in 401(k) plans are held in ESG funds, the report said.

The Labor Department proposal, which is subject to a 60-day public comment period, comes as the Trump administration has tried to make it easier for employers to add alternative investments such as private equity funds to workplace retirement plans.

It also comes amid a broader Trump administration backlash against ESG and policies it views as "woke."

Within days of his inauguration in 2025, President Donald Trump announced the U.S. would withdraw from the Paris Agreement, blocked subsidies for electric vehicles, pushed for more fossil-fuel production, and started a "huge pushback" against diversity, equity and inclusion policies, Diana Iovanel, a senior markets economist at Capital Economics, wrote in a research note last year.

"Our country will be woke no longer," Trump said during a speech to a joint session of Congress in March 2025.

More recently, Trump and the Republican-led Congress repealed key elements of the Inflation Reduction Act, former President Joe Biden's signature law to combat climate change. In April, a White House report said ESG investing has a "significant cost to investors and the broader economy."

Monday's proposal undoes elements of a Biden administration rule in 2022, Labor Department officials said. That Biden-era regulation sought to loosen ESG investing rules put in place in 2020 by the first Trump administration. The Biden rule, for example, clarified that employers could weigh the economic effects of climate change and other ESG considerations when making investment choices, officials said at the time.

Among the ways the latest proposal seeks to curb ESG uptake in 401(k) plans is by eliminating the "tiebreaker test" and the "participant preference provision," the Labor Department said.

The tiebreaker test says, for example, that if an ESG fund is "tied" with another fund after comparing various financial factors, a 401(k) plan sponsor can choose the ESG fund because of its social benefits, according to legal experts.

Current rules also allow employers to select funds based partly on their workers' preferences, provided they still meet certain financial criteria.

Data shows that ESG funds may provide stronger financial performance than that of more traditional funds, however.

For example, sustainable funds returned investors a median 4.9% in the first half of 2026, versus 4% for traditional funds, according to a Morgan Stanley analysis in September.

"Sustainable funds also continue to outperform over the long term: A hypothetical $100 investment into a sustainable fund in December 2018 — the start date for Morningstar's sustainable fund classification — would have grown to $171 today, while investing $100 into a traditional fund over the same period of time would have become $159 today, assuming median returns were achieved in each period," according to the report.
