#124. The rise and fall and rise again of Jensen Huang

It's being called a comeback for the ages.

For a long time it seemed that Jensen Huang, the leather jacket-clad founder of Nvidia, dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech", could do no wrong.

Then came allegations of two affairs - one with a businesswoman suspected of being a Chinese spy - that rocked the world of tech and the global stock market. Huang resigned.

Now, just 11 days later, he's been reinstated by the board, who says it believes he is the right person to lead the company.

So what do we know about the man who is once again at the head of Nvidia, a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China?

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession saw him amass an estimated fortune of close to $200bn earlier this year, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index, though that has since dropped to around $105bn.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on a par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while Huang is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

Huang admitted to having been unfaithful to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by an announcement that Huang was stepping down.

He'd barely been gone, however, before a "Bring Jensen Back" campaign was launched, reportedly by current and former Nvidia employees, partners, shareholders and supporters.

The man himself said he would be "honoured" to return, should the board ask him.

Although initially showing no interest in reinstating him, the board has now done just that, saying his return was "in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

#123. Pizza chains are under pressure. GLP-1 use is only one part of the problem

Big pizza chains are struggling as weaker consumer spending, a wider variety of dining options and the rise of GLP-1 medications weigh on their sales.

The pandemic was a boom time for Domino's Pizza, Papa Johns, Little Caesars and Pizza Hut. But since dining rooms reopened, the biggest U.S. pizza chains have faced an uphill battle to retain their customers.

These days, consumers are watching their discretionary spending but have more delivery and takeout options when they do decide to order, thanks to the accessibility of third-party platforms. Plus, food quality and the customer experience have become more important to diners who are looking for value when they order from restaurants.

As a result of pizza fatigue and challenges facing the broader restaurant industry, the number of U.S. pizza restaurants has tumbled. Since pizza locations hit a peak of 83,035 nationwide in August 2019, more than 9,600 pizzerias had closed their doors as of July 2026, according to data from Datassential.

In 2025, sales at U.S. pizza restaurants fell 0.3%, according to Technomic data. Sales at independent pizzerias slipped 0.4%. Chains, which account for about two-thirds of the domestic pizza category sales, saw revenue shrink 0.3%.

Papa Johns reported that North American same-store sales declined 2.5% in 2025. Pizza Hut's U.S. same-store sales fell 5%. Among the three pizza chains that were publicly traded last year, only Domino's posted annual same-store sales growth at its U.S. restaurants, with an increase of 3%.

It isn't just diners who have lost their appetite for pizza. Since March 2020, Papa Johns stock has lost about two-thirds of its value. Even shares of Domino's, which dominates the category, have seen gains of only about 2% in that time. For comparison, the S&P 500 has climbed about 150% over the same period.

And Yum Brands sold Pizza Hut this year, offloading the laggard of its portfolio to LongRange Capital and Yum China after about two decades of stagnating sales.

"Pizza Hut has been a detractor for YUM ... Going forward, YUM won't be tripped up by pizza category dynamics (discounting, etc.), translating to stronger growth and higher concentration in higher growth industry segments and markets," Wells Fargo analyst Zachary Fadem wrote in a research note in September.

Pizza chains now have significantly more competition for diners' dollars than they did in 2020. Third-party delivery platforms, which grew in popularity during the pandemic, offer a wide variety of cuisines beyond the traditional pizza and Chinese. As a result, Domino's and Papa John's have to compete with other fast-food chains, like McDonald's and Chick-fil-A, for DoorDash and Uber Eats orders.

As pizza has grown more expensive, a pie seems less appealing to budget-conscious diners. The average large cheese pizza is now almost $17, according to the 2026 Pizza Power Report from PMQ Pizza, a trade publication.

Plus, frozen pizza is another convenient option, with a lower price tag. Retail sales of single- and multi-serve frozen pizza rose 2.9% in the 52 weeks ended Nov. 7 compared with the same period in 2022, according to NielsenIQ data.

"When the American consumer is focused on the least expensive eating occasion, the items that are in my pantry, my refrigerator and my freezer win every single time," said David Portalatin, a food and food service industry advisor for Circana.

Convenience stores like Casey's General Stores have also won over customers with pizza at a low price point.

"We believe that [Casey's] has an advantage over [quick-service restaurants] and dollar stores, which have fewer options to navigate the persistent inflationary environment, while [Casey's] can offset food pressures with margins," Bank of America Securities analyst Sara Senatore wrote in a research note published in June.

She added that geographic overlap of locations suggests that Casey's is stealing share from Pizza Hut.

The biggest pizza chains have also been slow to adapt to shifting consumer attitudes toward nutrition. For example, of the four biggest chains, Papa Johns is the only one to even test a pizza crust with added protein as more diners prioritize it in their diets. And the test was only in one Atlanta restaurant — for a single day.

Pizza's nutritional content more broadly is another drag for the category. Its carbohydrate and fat content, from its dough and cheese, respectively, can make it less appealing for consumers on GLP-1 drugs, which have skyrocketed in use over the last several years.

Spending on pizza quick-service restaurants has fallen 22.2% per GLP-1 household after six to eight months using the medications, according to a 2026 survey published by PwC.

To be sure, pizza's recent struggles do not mean an existential crisis for the category. Total pizza servings are down only about 1% this year, Circana's Portalatin said.

And chains are trying to reverse that downturn in servings. In August, Domino's launched the Domino, a customizable Detroit-style personal pizza to attract solo diners. In November, Papa Johns unveiled a new turnaround plan focusing on improving food consistency and restaurant operations. And Pizza Hut is running a holiday-themed meal deal.

"Pizza's not going anywhere," Portalatin said. "The American consumer loves pizza."

#122. Trump sealed deal for Nvidia to rehire Jensen Huang as CEO

The stunning decision by Nvidia's board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO was sealed by a personal promise from President Donald Trump on Sunday that Huang had not broken national security laws, three people familiar with the board's thinking told CNBC.

Several other factors also set the stage for Huang's return and accelerated discussions with him over the weekend, said the people, who spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss internal deliberations close to Nvidia's board.

A sharp stock price drop after his resignation and questions about the leadership of interim CEO Colette Kress also spurred the board's decision, the people said.

"I convinced them," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a phone interview Monday morning, shortly after Nvidia announced Huang's return.

"I made the deal," the president said. "It was a lot easier to convince them yesterday than it was a week and a half ago.

Trump said Nvidia's board asked for guarantees that Huang, who resigned less than two weeks ago, was not a threat to national security.

"And we gave them assurances, at least with everything we know," the president said. "We believe he's fine. He's absolutely fine."

Senior Trump administration officials reached out to the board overnight Friday to inform them that Huang had not violated U.S. national security laws, people familiar with the board's actions said.

After the White House outreach, the board contacted Huang, who joined meetings on Saturday and Sunday to discuss his potential return, the people said.

Huang's spokesman did not respond to a request for comment when asked about his contact with the board and other events leading up to his return.

The Nvidia founder resigned on Nov. 12 after reports that he had an affair with an Nvidia employee and with Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singapore-based company Megaspeed who is suspected of being a spy for China. A report of an affair with a second Nvidia employee broke right after he quit.

Nvidia's board named Kress, the company's chief financial officer, as the interim CEO while it searched for a permanent successor.

But within days of Kress taking over, some employees and investors who spoke to CNBC questioned her stewardship, as many of them clamored for Huang's return.

Kress' performances at an all-hands company meeting on Wednesday and on Nvidia's earnings call with analysts a day earlier were seen by some investors as uninspiring and underwhelming.

As the board faced those concerns amid a stock drop of more than 25% over the past week, Huang was aggressively working the phones with major tech CEOs who wanted to see him return to Nvidia, according to sources close to Huang.

In the days after he resigned, Huang spoke with OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, OpenAI President Greg Brockman, and Elon Musk, CEO of SpaceX and Tesla, one of the sources said.

Huang wanted his job back, but he was not sure what the return path looked like, the sources said.

On Nov. 17, less than a week after quitting, Huang issued a public statement saying he was open to returning as CEO.

"I've seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA's employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO," he said.

"NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company," he added. "If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO."

On the same day, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, Harvey Jones, told analysts on the company's earnings call that the board did "not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

Kress, the following day, told company employees at the all-hands meeting, "Let's move forward."

Nvidia's public stance against Huang returning came as members of Congress moved ahead with plans to interview him at hearings and as criticism grew over the company's business in Asia.

The Department of Justice and the Commerce Department, in an unusual joint public statement on Nov. 17, said they were investigating Megaspeed for possible diversion of advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export control law.

"Sensitive technologies like semiconductors are among the most valuable and strategic assets of the U.S. economy," the departments said.

But days later, senior Trump administration officials contacted Nvidia's board to tell them that Jensen Huang himself was not a risk, according to the sources.

The Department of Commerce added Megaspeed to a trade blacklist later Monday.

Trump, during his interview Monday with CNBC, was asked what Attorney General Todd Blanche had told him about potential criminal liability for Huang and the question of whether Nvidia chips had ended up in China in violation of U.S. export laws.

"They did a pretty good investigation, and they felt that he, himself, was exonerated," Trump said.

Both Trump and Vice President JD Vance became convinced that Huang was a singular figure who is preventing closed-source-model AI companies, such as Anthropic, from getting too powerful," according to a person familiar with the matter, who spoke on condition of anonymity to disclose private discussions.

The person said Trump and Vance were briefed on the national security elements of the situation and saw no evidence that Huang did anything illegal or untoward in that regard.

Trump believes his administration's close working relationship with Huang gives the U.S. its best opportunity to protect national security and prevent cutting-edge AI chips from reaching banned customers, according to the person.

Trump said he spoke with Huang on Sunday, after Nvidia had voted unanimously to reinstate him.

"I called him up, and I said: 'I ask you to do one thing, Jensen. Work hard,'" Trump said.

"He knows what to do and what not to do," Trump said. "But I just said work hard because if you work hard, there's nobody that can capture what you've done. Nobody, nobody close."

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger and Kif Leswing contributed to this article.

#120. How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Nvidia and its co-founder Jensen Huang have been at the centre of the artificial intelligence (AI) spending boom.

No company or chief executive has arguably been more consequential to the development of AI in recent years, with the US microchip manufacturer under Huang positioning itself as a critical cog in the development of the world-changing technology.

That central position prompted questions, in the aftermath of Huang's departure over revelations of extramarital affairs, over how important he was to the wider AI industry and US economy.

The fact he has been handed his job back after just 11 days in a U-turn by Nvidia's board implies the top brass believe Huang is irreplaceable.

Shares in the company have surged on the news of Huang's shock return, following a period of sharp declines.

While Nvidia is not as much of a household name as other Big Tech players such as Apple, Amazon and Microsoft, the company has been just as integral to the booming US stock market of recent years.

The billions of dollars being funnelled into AI and the construction of data centres has bolstered the American economy, with Nvidia being one of the biggest spenders.

Originally focusing on chips which process graphics for computer games, it pivoted to making the chips used in AI systems and now dominates the market.

It is now one the most valuable firms in the world and its rapid growth is poised to continue as the firm's quarterly sales doubled from a year earlier.

Huang played a major role in the company, leading its shift towards AI, and maintained a firm grip on it, with up to 200 people directly reporting to him.

Some suggested his departure, which was sparked by the emergence that Huang had an affair with a woman alleged to have been a spy for China, posed a risk to the wider AI industry.

That was because it was unclear whether Nvidia would change strategy and potential rein in spending - a move could have had wider implications for the US economy, but which looks unlikely now Huang has returned.

The fears were sparked due to Nvidia being a key source of finance for many firms, as well being a microchip supplier. On its journey to the top of the AI industry, it has, through Huang, created a spider's web of deals through what is known as "circular financing".

Not only does it sell microchips to its customers, which include the likes of Meta, SpaceX and OpenAI, it has also provided funds for them and other firms in the supply chain by either handing out loans or investing in them directly.

The practice has prompted concerns among some investors, who worry what might happen if Nvidia were to turn off the spending taps. If investment dried up, would other firms in the supply chain cut spending or jobs themselves? Would that spread to the wider US economy?

That risk was perhaps why tech bosses including Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, the CEO of Fortnite maker Epic Games, had called for Huang's reinstatement.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation, and backed a campaign, along with shareholders and supporters, to "Bring Jensen Back".

Following the announcement of Huang's return, Musk posted: "The mistake has been corrected."

Kathleen Brooks, research director at investment platform XTB, said Huang's return suggested he was "the godfather" of AI and has the status to shift "the dial for corporate finance".

"Is Jensen Huang the most valuable man on the planet? His return suggests more of the same when it comes to circular financing and the rapid AI infrastructure build out, and the market loves it," she added.

However, Huang's return to Nvidia does not solve the wider issues rattling the tech stock markets in recent weeks.

Investors had taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will actually pay off.

However, investment in AI is still expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, who said the technology would remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

The value of the shares in Big Tech companies have cushioned the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, buoying investments and pensions for people worldwide.

President Donald Trump hailed Huang as a "great patriot and national treasure" after his reappointment and pointed to the chief executive's wider impact.

"The country needs Jensen's leadership at Nvidia in order to ensure the success and prosperity of not only the company, but the American economy," he wrote on Truth Social.

Nvidia said its leadership had received input from "senior levels of the United States government and was "confident that Mr Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk".

#118. Nvidia value climbs by almost $1tn and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

A comeback for the ages: Trump's influence on Huang's return to Nvidia

Nvidia's market value rose by nearly $1 trillion as its share price skyrocketed after the tech firm reinstated Jensen Huang as its chief executive with immediate effect.

His shock reinstatement comes less than two weeks after Huang resigned after admitting an affair with a woman who was alleged to be a Chinese spy, a period in which shares in the chipmaker have also fallen significantly.

Nvidia shares rose by more than 31% which also lifted stock markets, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq climbing by 6.5% and the S&P 500 also rose 4.75%.

Tech stocks more broadly have also rallied following large falls on Friday, with much of the rally starting in pre-market trading before Huang's return was announced.

Other chipmakers experienced large increases following Monday's announcement from the Nvidia board. Shares in Micron Technology jumped 19%, while AMD shares rose nearly 7%.

Tesla shares also lept up by nearly 11.5%, while other tech gains were more muted by comparison: Amazon shares were up nearly 3.5%, and Apple and Microsoft rose by less than 2%.

While the surge in Nvidia shares was large and drove its market value back towards $4tn, the firm's share price remains about 21% down from a month ago.

A sharp drop occurred on 4 November, after the Democrat's midterm election victory sparked fears of tougher AI regulation to come led to a broad tech stock sell-off.

On Friday alone, Nvidia shares fell by 13.7% as tech stocks more broadly fell, and as Reuters reported - unconfirmed by the BBC - that the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) over its accounting practices.

Nvidia is a key firm at the core of the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, with the chips it makes central to the industry.

Nvidia's board of directors voted unanimously to reinstate the firm's co-founder following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him, the company said - but US President Donald Trump has claimed he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang.

Kathleen Brooks, research director at brokerage firm XTB, said Huang's return showed Huang was the "godfather" of AI, and "has a talismanic influence over the sector".

"Barely two weeks ago this man was ousted from the company that he founded due to serious misconduct and potential national security issues," she said.

"Even though Huang did not hold special voting shares on the board, Nvidia's directors cannot see a future for the company without him."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said Huang's reinstatement was an astonishing about-turn for Nvidia, particularly given reports of an as-yet unconfirmed SEC investigation.

"This is the biggest corporate U-turn in living memory. To go from being embroiled in a spy scandal to being reinstated as the head of one of the world's most valuable companies in less than a fortnight is nothing short of remarkable," he said.

"Today's stock market rebound represents a massive sigh of relief by investors. However, one cannot rule out a more conservative approach by Huang going forward.

"His every move will be scrutinised by the market, and he may wish to tread more carefully to protect his integrity and ensure that Nvidia is being run responsibly."

Politicians are also demanding greater scrutiny.

Senator Jeanne Shaheen, the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, said Huang's reinstatement "reportedly at the encouragement of Trump Administration officials and without any apparent accounting or response to the risks to our national security, demands an explanation."

She added: "What is abundantly clear is that the AI industry requires guardrails and oversight."

#117. An options strategy for making even more of your money back in Nvidia

Nvidia's board has officially reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, igniting a roughly 28% relief rally into the low-$160s after last week's brutal sell-off drove shares down to $125.53. While Huang's return removes a major leadership vacuum, the stock still sits far below its $243.37 52-week high and autumn trading ranges above $200.

Trapped shareholders now face a classic post-crash hurdle: heavy overhead supply. Investors who bought near prior peaks often use sharp rebounds to exit at breakeven, creating stubborn technical resistance between $175 and $210 — especially with congressional testimony and export scrutiny still lingering.

Rather than waiting passively for a full V-shaped surge to all-time highs, underwater holders can deploy a stock repair strategy geared toward a partial recovery.

For every 100 shares of NVDA held, an investor can overlay a January 17/195 1x2 call ratio spread for a net debit of $1.10 ($110 per lot):

Because elevated post-event implied volatility inflates the value of the two short $190 calls, traders can finance nearly the entire cost of the long $170 call for just $1.10 out of pocket. This structure acts as a leveraged recovery accelerator across the 175-to-195 resistance zone without adding margin risk, since one short call is covered by the existing shares and the other by the long $175 call. At January expiration, three outcomes govern the position:

The trade-off is capped upside above $214.90, making the strategy ideal for shareholders who view 195–215 as a realistic medium-term ceiling rather than a mere pit stop.

#115. International first class is over for U.S. airlines. Enter the mega business-class cabin

The era of U.S. airlines' long-haul, international first class ended this week when American Airlines' last flight with its eight-suite section landed. In its place will be a massive business-class cabin with room for 70 passengers.

U.S. airlines have been ditching international first-class cabins for a new top tier: larger and souped-up business classes, featuring suites with sliding doors, the design du jour. Delta Air Lines and United Airlines have already taken this step. American was the last of the U.S. airlines to still have first class on long-haul international routes.

Many travelers might not notice the difference. That's because of airlines' investments into business class: new suites, bigger screens, better technology like device-charging pads, and improved bedding.

It's part of airlines' big bet on mass luxury. Consumers have been willing to shell out more to fly comfortably, which is helping to boost airline revenue. That's all the more important as costs of everything from fuel to airport landing fees have been rising, soaking up more of the industry's profits.

The retirement of first class ends a more-than-six-decade stretch of the jet age that in the early days included service like seat-side carving of chateaubriand and meals served on fine bone china.

A few decades ago, business class was much smaller, with two-by-two seats in some configurations, and recliners, not the lie-flat seats that dominate those cabins now.

"When you've got individual pods in biz class, and you've got a comfortable enough seat and bedding and decent wine, for a lot of people this is good enough," said Henry Harteveldt, founder of travel consulting firm Atmosphere Research Group and a former airline executive.

The over-the-top dining of the early days of international first class was a form of entertainment, long before laptops, personal seat-back screens and in-flight Wi-Fi, or even the promise of sleep on lie-flat beds, but customers still eat lots on board, Harteveldt said.

"People say they want to sleep but then they stay up and they eat every morsel," he said. "They're hypocrites."

There was still a hefty price gap between first and business.

A flight between Los Angeles and London on American's Boeing 777-300ER this week went for $12,994 in business class for a one-way ticket and $15,669 for a first-class seat.

American's last flight with international first class departed Friday morning local time in Sydney, bound for Los Angeles.

Its new cabin on its Boeing 777-300ERs will feature 70 business-class suites, the most of any U.S. airline. Pre-overhaul, the cabins had been operating with eight first-class suites and 52 pods in business, though the remodeling is ongoing. American is also flying a subfleet of 787-9 Dreamliners that have 51 business-class suites. (United is putting 64 new suites on that aircraft.)

Part of the allure is that some companies allow employees to book business-class tickets, but not first class.

American Airlines told CNBC it's growing its business-class cabins across the fleet. It's evaluating increasing the number of suites on its smaller long-haul jets, the Boeing 787-8 Dreamliners, from 20 currently to somewhere in the 30s.

"You don't have enough seats to make the seat cost competitive," Nat Pieper, American's chief commercial officer, said in an interview this month. "So what you need to do to make that airplane work is it's got to be a yield play. You need more business-class seats."

Differences between first and business classes on American lately came down to details.

On American's first-class long-haul section, passengers got olives with warm nuts to accompany their beverage early in the flight, while there were no olives for business class.

First class got a soup; business did not. First class got a warm dessert served a la mode in addition to the cold dessert or fruit and cheese plate both cabins were offered.

And there was no memory foam lumbar pillow for business class like there was in first, American said. But otherwise, the experiences were fairly similar for passengers. They still got better food options and service than economy passengers, along with their bigger seats, early boarding and lounge access.

"It's not like the bed can go any flatter than 180 degrees," Harteveldt said. "The bedding can be thicker, and the food nicer."

Delta and United have already replaced international, long-haul first class with dozens of their Delta One and Polaris business lie-flat suites, respectively, each of which is getting upgrades now.

American will continue offering a separate lie-flat first class on its Airbus A321T subfleet it uses for domestic cross-country flying until early 2027. But then that goes away, too.

Meanwhile, airlines over the past decade have added a premium economy section, which sits between business class and economy. It features recliner seats similar to those in domestic first class, often with foot rests, meals served on actual plates, larger seat-back screens than economy and earlier boarding.

Airlines are now cutting up business class into different segments, something they've done for more than a decade with the coach cabin's basic economy, standard and extra legroom seats.

United and Delta this year each announced their most expensive cabins will be sold with basic options, which are more restrictive and don't include things like the ability to pick your seat ahead of time or get access to a special airport lounge.

There are also a few seats in some of the business-class cabins that are roomier, like American's Flagship Suite Preferred and United's Polaris Studio. Even the guest seating in the United suite varies with "standard and companion ottomans," the latter including a seatbelt so you can ask "another Polaris passenger to visit you between takeoff and landing for a drink, chat or game," the airline says.

"I don't think we found the limit on segmentation," United CFO Mike Leskinen told CNBC on Thursday. "We have a few things on the drawing board. We're not done."

Although U.S. carriers are moving away from the designation, first class is far from dead.

Singapore Airlines, Germany's Lufthansa, British Airways and Air France have unveiled updated first-class cabins and other service improvements like food.

British Airways last month said it will build a record 106-suite business class on its Airbus A380 aircraft, the world's largest passenger airplane, but it's also upgrading its first class on board.

Meanwhile, smaller airlines like JetBlue Airways, Frontier Airlines and Allegiant Air are introducing domestic or short-haul first-class seats.

"We're going to be able to do that at a really low price comparatively to what you're used to out there in the marketplace," Frontier Chief Commercial Officer Bobby Schroeter said last month.

#114. Faisal Islam: Why Jensen Huang proved too big to fire

'Biggest U-turn in economic history': Experts react to Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia

There have been three jaw-dropping moments in the past few hours around Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia chief executive after he admitted an affair with a woman who was alleged to be a Chinese spy.

The shock announcement of his return to the chipmaker was followed by the addition of one trillion dollars of stock market value to Nvidia.

Then it was the moment President Trump admitted the obvious: that it was his "You're unfired" message direct to the board that changed the decision.

The nature of the U-turn is clear from what Nvidia was saying just last week. The board was clear that Huang was not returning.

The interim management had also suggested some shifts in strategy away from the Huang era.

All of that is now in the dustbin of corporate history.

This is a comeback that exceeds the return of Sam Altman to OpenAI or Steve Jobs to Apple, in terms of sheer numbers.

LIVE: Nvidia shares soar after Jensen Huang returns as CEO

The rise and fall and rise again of Jensen Huang

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

The reason for the turnaround was pretty clear in the first statement. The US government had said there were no national security concerns with his reinstatement. Such a reassurance could only have come from the White House.

And an hour later President Trump revealed that he had spoken directly to the Nvidia board.

This is where I go back to my own conversation with Jensen Huang last September, where he openly advocated for allowing Chinese access to advanced Nvidia microchips. He was one of the few people in the world who could credibly say he knew the minds of both President Trump and President Xi.

Reports in the US say there were splits in the Trump administration between China hawks wanting to restrict exports, and others who backed Huang's argument.

There is still an independent investigation into the company's compliance with export controls.

Nvidia's chips are critical to the development of frontier artificial intelligence systems. Jensen Huang's influence over this pipeline meant he was more important than many world leaders.

This is a move of importance not just to the flagging US tech stock boom, but the future of the world economy, of US politics, and of course the geopolitical race between the US and China.

The recent doubts about Nvidia struck at the heart of the AI boom. This is after all a company actually generating phenomenal amounts of real sales - over $400bn (Â£305bn) this year, compared with $27bn four years ago.

Nvidia's stock market valuation has been used as a critical form of financing for other companies to construct data centres.

A complex web of loans, guarantees, and shareholdings helped spread and sustain the AI boom. Jensen Huang's exit had raised doubts about the fragility of this system, and the interim management had suggested a more "disciplined and selective" approach.

The concerns about so-called "circular financing" still raise concerns from some analysts. The public backlash against datacentres and AI evident at the US midterm elections also point to a changed environment.

The Democrats in Congress have vowed to investigate both the President and Mr Huang, and he is due to appear before them next week. Jensen Huang is at the centre of it all - the technology, the financing, the US politics and the geopolitics.

Today has proven Jensen Huang was in fact, too big to fire.

Nvidia shares up 30% and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

#112. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: AI race spreads from robots to smartphones

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

I'm just back from APEC in Shenzhen, where I saw the leaders of the U.S., China and Russia gather along with other member economies. Beneath the fanfare, what's changed on the tech front in what's often dubbed China's Silicon Valley?

It only took a few months for a humanoid robot to get better at picking up household items.

Even after a human knocked a box and toys away from their original spots, a LimX robot quickly located them and moved them to pre-set locations. While still far from perfect, the smoothness of the demo contrasted with the robot's more stilted movements I'd seen early this summer — and inability then to react to rapid changes in tasks.

When we caught up in Shenzhen last week, CFO Jesse Liu declined to comment on LimX's IPO plans. But he told me that his conversations around the APEC CEO Summit revealed an excitement around a new development for humanoids: growing demand for robot chips that can run smarter large language models locally, instead of on the cloud.

"A lot of chipmakers are now happy about the latest developments in LLMs because they think robotic OEMs will actually begin to integrate more storage, and reasoning, and compute, and inference infrastructure on the edge, or locally inside the robot body," Liu said, referring to original equipment manufacturers.

"They do see robots moving from hundreds of units to thousands of units, to tens of thousands of units, beginning to enter that kind of automotive quantity," he said, pointing to chip suppliers such as Horizon Robotics.

New AI models, particularly GPT-6 Astra, have shown not just improved reasoning, but better control of robots.

That puts more of the onus on hardware makers, for robots or other devices, to create practical applications. LimX said it's working with business partners in over 30 markets, including Switzerland where the robots serve as power plant inspectors in rural areas.

The key for powering AI's transition into the physical world is on-device chips and more powerful batteries. That means Nvidia's most expensive, cutting-edge chips won't be the only ones powering generative AI models.

So far, even in basic consumer applications such as smartphones, AI isn't transformative enough to be a top-three selling point for mass-market users, Thomas Bai, AI product expert at smartphone company Honor, told me in Shenzhen.

He expects that in the next few years, as smartphone chips are able to run more powerful AI models, the "super agent" tech can become better at completing tasks on its own, and anticipating a user's needs — such as ordering coffee.

Counterpoint on Nov. 19 forecast that in the retail industry, "edge AI" revenue will surge sixfold by 2032, with roughly half of revenue coming from retail robots that clean and check inventory — often with the help of smaller AI models that can run on locally embedded chips.

But more capable, on-device AI also requires more battery power, Honor's Bai pointed out.

Honor's X70 Android smartphone, which launched last summer, boasts a battery with 8,300 milliampere-hours for a total device weight of just under 200 grams. That compares with 5,000 mAH for the 214-gram flagship Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra, and roughly 5,400 mAH for this year's 249-gram Apple iPhone 18 Pro Max, according to GSM Arena.

LimX's Oli humanoid comes with 9,500 mAH battery capacity, lasting the robot about 1.5 hours.

Such AI applications, however, remain sensitive to the memory-chip shortage weighing on the industry.

The X70 was the best-selling Android phone in the entry to mid-tier range, said Shenzhen-based Counterpoint analyst Shiwen Ma.

While Ma said penetration of smartphones with generative AI features is higher in China at 55% versus 38% overseas, the offerings are still not essential to users, while rising memory-chip costs make it harder for manufacturers to bring AI-powered devices to the mass market.

Geely-backed automotive tech company EcarX's CFO Dylan Jeng told me earlier this month he expects the memory price surge to persist through 2027, and even the first half of 2028.

The chipmaker that can narrow the gap for on-device AI needs could find its moment soon.

Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke exclusively on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week.

How China is using summits to expand its global influence

For a second year in a row, Xi has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores. But U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returned from Asia.

Putin scores 'massive win' at first trilateral meeting

The first-ever three-way summit is "arguably a diplomatic victory for Moscow," said Andrius Tursa, an advisor for Central and Eastern Europe and Russia at Teneo. The view from inside the APEC welcome banquet showed the leaders were in close proximity from the start of the summit.

#109. Jim Cramer sees more upside for Nvidia stock with Jensen Huang back as CEO

Nvidia's remarkable stock comeback isn't done yet now that Jensen Huang has returned as CEO, CNBC's Jim Cramer said Monday.

"It's terrific to have this admittedly tarnished national treasure back leading Nvidia," the "Mad Money" host said. "It's kind of a miracle given the board's insistence that he would never get the CEO job again. But it's a miracle that saved the Nasdaq which, in turn, saved the market, at least for now."

Nvidia shares soared 31.5% on the news, fueling a broader rally in AI infrastructure stocks. The S&P 500 jumped 4.7%, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite surged 6.5%, with both indexes posting their best days since April 2025. Monday's rebound erased nearly all of Nvidia's decline following Huang's Nov. 12 resignation amid reports of multiple extramarital affairs, including one involving an alleged Chinese spy. Before the rally Monday, the stock had fallen in all trading sessions since his departure.

"Jensen's only one man, and Nvidia is only one company," he said. "But the truth is that Jensen is the linchpin that holds the AI solar system together, and Nvidia is the biggest beneficiary."

Cramer argued that Huang's reinstatement removes a major source of uncertainty for investors, strengthening the case for a higher valuation. The chipmaker delivered blockbuster quarterly results last week, raising its revenue growth outlook to 75%, but the results did little to stem the sell-off as investors remained concerned about its leadership.

"If it weren't for the Jensen overhang, and the insistence that Jensen could never come back, ... I think the stock would have been up significantly on that quarter," Cramer said.

Still, Cramer cautioned that Huang's return doesn't erase the broader headwinds facing Nvidia. Growing political opposition to data center construction following the midterm elections could slow AI infrastructure expansion, while uncertainty surrounding chip sales to China threatens a potential source of demand. Meanwhile, a more hawkish Federal Reserve has pressured technology stock valuations as investors brace for higher interest rates.

Those challenges, Cramer argued, make it difficult to justify a return to the $213 level Nvidia traded at before The New York Times first reported about Huang's relationship with a woman suspected of ties to Chinese intelligence on Oct. 29. But with Huang back at the helm and the company's growth outlook intact, he said the stock deserves a higher valuation. The stock currently trades at roughly 10 times earnings estimates for Nvidia's fiscal 2028, according to FactSet.

"There is just no way that, with the return of Jensen, this stock deserves the same 10 times price to earnings multiple," Cramer said.

"These things are subjective, of course, but to start, let's say this stock deserves to trade at twelve times next year's earnings estimate, which would make this a $199 stock," Cramer said. "That's my target."

#105. Fed's John Williams indicates higher interest rates likely 'over time' to address inflation

New York Federal Reserve President John Williams said Monday that policymakers have more work to do to bring inflation under control, though he indicated that further action can unfold over time as the economy continues to grow.

In remarks consistent with previous statements, the influential central banker expressed confidence that inflation will get closer to the Fed's 2% target in 2027 before hitting the goal the following year. He also said that while artificial intelligence is pressuring prices, there's "little evidence" it will be the source of more persistent inflation.

"But getting inflation back to 2% is 100% essential," he said in remarks at the University of Mount Saint Vincent in New York. "We must take actions over time that will ensure that we get inflation back to our 2% goal in a timely manner and on a sustained basis. There is still work to do to get that done."

The comments come at a delicate moment for monetary policy, as the economy continues to grow and inflation stays well above the Fed's goal.

Fed officials have differed over how quickly to raise rates, with some advocating more aggressive action while others, including Williams, have emphasized a measured approach.

Williams ascribed the current inflation pressures to the conflict in Iran, demand for goods associated with the AI build-out and tariffs, the effects of which he said "have largely abated" as a source of pressure. Nevertheless, he expressed confidence that inflation will subside substantially in 2027 even as economic growth remains robust and unemployment edges lower.

The Federal Open Market Committee next meets on Dec. 8-9, with markets fully pricing in a quarter percentage point interest rate increase from the current target range of 3.75%-4%. The remarks from Williams are expected to be the last major policy statement from any Fed official before the December decision.

"Monetary policy cannot move ships or reopen pipelines and refineries, but it can — and must — ensure that these shocks do not lead to broader or persistent inflationary dynamics or erode well-anchored inflation expectations," Williams said, according to his prepared remarks.

Williams did not explicitly address the December meeting expectations, instead pointing to the statement released after the prior FOMC meeting in late October that said the "accumulation of more information" will help decide the future policy path.

"The data that we have received since then show that the economy has remained strong and that inflation has continued to run too high," he said.

#102. Employees at Eric Trump-backed robot startup lose health benefits as company's cash crunch deepens

Employees at Foundation Future Industries were told that their health benefits through administrator Rippling have been terminated, according to a memo obtained by CNBC, adding to mounting signs of financial distress at the humanoid robot startup advised and backed by Eric Trump.

Foundation told staff in a Friday evening email that they'd be offered coverage through COBRA, the federal law that generally lets workers temporarily keep their health insurance after losing benefits by paying the full premium out of pocket.

"Because our last pay run through Rippling was in October, they have ended our benefits with them retroactive to October," a Foundation human resources manager said in the memo. "COBRA will be offered to all employees to cover November."

Foundation has garnered headlines for the bold claims that co-founder Sankaet Pathak — whose previous startup collapsed in 2024 — has made about his robots and their potential use in war zones. Employees were last paid on Oct. 15, and some inside the company are angry at Pathak for a $4.6 million loan he took from his company last year, CNBC reported earlier this month.

A Foundation spokesman didn't immediately respond to an email or phone call seeking comment.

In the staff memo, Foundation blamed Rippling for the disruption to health coverage. The startup also said it would reimburse COBRA premiums paid by workers and that it was onboarding a new benefits provider.

But employees, especially those whose family members are undergoing treatment for medical ailments, worry the company won't have funds to reimburse workers or reinstate coverage, people with direct knowledge of the situation said.

In a statement to CNBC, Rippling indicated it was Foundation's failure to pay for its employee benefits that led to the change: "If a client enrolled in Rippling's [Professional Employer Organization] is unable to meet their payroll and benefits obligations for multiple months, they will first receive a warning and if not met, they are terminated."

Foundation has been seeking a bridge loan to repay employees after its financial woes began in August. It has been in talks with three or four venture capital firms, including existing investor Tribe Capital, Pathak has told employees, according to people familiar with the situation, who asked to remain unnamed because they were not authorized to speak publicly and feared retaliation. Tribe didn't immediately return a request for comment.

The bridge loan would help the company survive until a possible $150 million investment from South Korean conglomerate Base Group lands as early as December or January, Pathak has told employees, the people said.

Base confirmed to Reuters in recent days that it was considering an investment, but the funding has not been finalized.

The potential investment would deepen business ties between the Trump family and the South Korean conglomerate, an aluminum producer also known for real estate and hospitality projects.

Base paid the Trump Organization $2 million in 2025, according to a New York Times article, drawing scrutiny from Democratic senators over conflict-of-interest concerns. Base has also pursued other business relationships with the Trump Organization, including a Trump-branded golf course in South Korea.

Eric Trump, who has invested in Foundation and was given the title of chief strategy advisor, didn't immediately respond to a CNBC email seeking comment.

The prospect of employees having to pay for their own health coverage comes as many are already drawing down savings to cover living expenses. With another scheduled payday approaching on Nov. 30, workers are facing a third straight pay cycle without income.

Many are actively interviewing for other jobs, and attendance at the company's San Francisco headquarters has fallen sharply, according to people familiar.

Frustration has boiled over into distrust of Pathak's leadership for some employees, while others hope Pathak can secure funding to repay workers and bring the company back to financial health. In conversations and memos, Pathak says that he is highly invested in securing Foundation's future, the people said.

Despite the firm's shaky finances, Foundation continued to hire for open roles until recently and some new workers have never been fully paid, said the people.

To keep employees from leaving before a possible Base Group investment, Pathak has floated the idea of offering workers equity in the startup, the people said.

At the same time, human resources staff have warned workers against taking legal action with California to recover their wages, saying that any employees who file complaints with the state would be dismissed, the people said.

Following a Nov. 14 CNBC report detailing the missed payrolls and a $4.6 million loan Pathak took from the startup in 2025, the chief executive addressed the issue in an all-hands meeting, according to the people.

Pathak defended the arrangement by telling workers that he had plowed his own money into the startup and takes only a nominal salary, the people said.

The co-founder also told employees who were in dire financial straits to contact human resources for help covering personal bills, the people added.

"I'm burning through savings just to afford my apartment," one employee told CNBC. "It's extremely unfortunate we're in this situation."

#99. Congress shreds Nvidia's 'outrageous' decision to bring back Huang: 'Failure of character'

Members of Congress from both parties on Monday expressed outrage and incredulity, and doubled down on their calls for investigations, after Nvidia announced it has reinstated Jensen Huang as its president and CEO.

The resurrection of Huang, who had resigned from the artificial intelligence behemoth just 11 days earlier, raises more questions about the company's decision-making and the Trump administration's national security assessments, on top of existing concerns about Nvidia's advanced AI chips being diverted to China, the lawmakers said.

"It's inexplicable to me why they want this guy back as CEO," Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., a China hawk who is retiring from Congress, told CNBC in a phone interview. "To me it's a failure of character."

President Donald Trump told CNBC in an extended interview later Monday morning that he personally intervened to convince Nvidia's board to bring Huang back, saying, "I made the deal."

Bacon, chairman of the House Subcommittee on Cyber, Information Technologies, and Innovation, said he wants the next Congress to conduct thorough oversight, while questioning the Trump administration's motives.

"Somebody's responsible for this, and it may be" Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose department is responsible for setting U.S. export controls on AI chips, Bacon said. "But I think we should get to the bottom of this."

Huang, who is scheduled to testify before multiple committees on Capitol Hill starting Dec. 1, is "going to get high scrutiny in a bipartisan way when he comes next week," Bacon said.

Huang's closed-door appearance before a trio of House committees is on the schedule, as the panels seek answers on the national security implications of Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, MS NOW reported. The Senate banking committee announced after Huang's reinstatement that he will appear for a public hearing Dec. 3.

John Fisher of Trident, a spokesman for Huang, declined to comment on the Nvidia CEO's scheduled appearances.

"He better be there," Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., a member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told CNBC about Huang's scheduled Dec. 1 appearance. His reinstatement will only intensify congressional probes into Nvidia and its government connections, Amo predicted.

"I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query, and it makes Howard Lutnick and his dealings — supporting his nepo children's operation at Cantor Fitzgerald — party to inquiry as well as any other administration official that has facilitated a status quo in which chips can be sold to China or Chinese operated intermediaries who don't intend good things for the American people," said Amo, referring to the financial firm run by Lutnick's sons that has business ties to Nvidia.

A Cantor spokesperson didn't comment directly when asked about Amo's comments, saying the firm "holds no material proprietary position in Nvidia" and noting its Nvidia positions "arise primarily from equity swaps executed for clients."

The Commerce Department didn't immediately respond to requests to comment on Bacon's and Amo's remarks.

Nvidia in a statement Monday said it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident that Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

David Rudenstine, a constitutional law professor and national security scholar at Cardozo School of Law, said the frenzy around Huang's comeback is secondary to lingering national security concerns."The real responsibility for national security is with the administration and not with the CEO of a private company," Rudenstine told CNBC."You're opposed to China getting the chips. You recognize that the intermediary companies are sending chips to China, and you don't put a stop to it. I don't get it," he said.

The CEO's reinstatement, while celebrated by the White House and in Silicon Valley, led to heightened calls on Capitol Hill to seek answers on AI chip exports to China and what the Trump administration's role has been in facilitating that.

"We are sending high-end computer chips to China, and yet we're saying we want to win the AI war. It's a contradiction, and it's a failure of leadership in the White House," Bacon told CNBC.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, told CNBC in an emailed statement that Huang's return "proves that under Trump, Vance, and Rubio, national security is for sale to the highest bidder."

"We will all pay the price of letting China plunder America's cutting edge technology. Democrats will be pushing for accountability for anyone in the administration that put corrupt deals over our country's security," Wyden, who is also a Senate Intelligence Committee member, said.

Trump in a Truth Social post Monday added that Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

Lawmakers, who have already launched multiple investigations related to Huang, Nvidia and related entities, quickly filed in to question those conclusions.

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., a close Trump ally and a former CEO, criticized the move and called for a congressional briefing "as soon as possible" on Huang's reinstatement.

"Nvidia's board is free to vote however they want, but the federal government owes the American people answers about what actually happened," Scott told CNBC in an emailed statement. "This looks terrible on its face, and it drives home the need for Congress to be informed on the full impact of Jensen Huang's actions on our national security and economy."

Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., a member of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, told CNBC that it's still not clear to what degree Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy might have compromised national security.

"The fact that the president of the United States is involved in putting him back in there, presumably because maybe he has stock or he just cares about the stock market, it's really reached a bizarre place," Peters said. "And obviously that man has not learned anything from the election about what people think of him, or he doesn't care."

Monday's move also gave fuel to lawmakers who have already questioned Trump, his administration or Nvidia.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement via the Senate Banking Committee, where she is the top Democrat and is expected to become chair in January.

Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, more tersely questioned the official assurances that Huang poses no risks to national security.

"No national security risk? Based on what?" he wrote on X.

Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi, D-Ill., who serves on the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, in an X post called Nvidia's decision to reinstate Huang "outrageous."

"We still don't know what information Chinese intelligence may have obtained from him, and neither the government nor Nvidia's own board has completed its investigation," wrote Krishnamoorthi, who is also a member of the House Oversight and Intelligence panels.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan, Garrett Downs and Luke Fountain contributed to this report.

#98. ESPN, NFL to show Super Bowl 61 in Imax theaters

Super Bowl 61 will be telecast live in hundreds of Imax theaters in February via a partnership with the NFL and Disney's ESPN, which has the broadcast rights to the 2027 game.

It marks the largest Imax live event in the company's history and the first time that the Super Bowl will be available in the format, according to a news release.

The four-hour event on Feb. 14 will include ESPN's main Super Bowl game telecast, as well as Jay-Z's halftime performance and pregame and postgame coverage.

Tickets will be available for theaters in the United States and select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil. Tickets are expected to go on sale in January. Pricing was not disclosed.

"Watching the Super Bowl with your fellow fans is one of the great shared experiences in sports, and this first-of-its-kind collaboration with the NFL and IMAX takes it to a historic new scale," said Ken Potrock, president of major event integration for The Walt Disney Co.

In addition to ESPN channels, this season's championship game will also be available on streaming platform Disney+ as well as Disney's broadcast network ABC.

#94. Peterson: Here’s what Bessent’s about-face on Jensen Huang reveals about Trump and China

For a minute there, it seemed like someone in the Trump administration might actually be willing to stand up to China.

For about a week and a half, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was the face of an emerging harder line on Nvidia over the potential risks its co-founder Jensen Huang's affairs posed to national security. Bessent was giving voice to China hawks who had felt sidelined by the Trump administration's willingness to sell artificial intelligence chips to  China.

They see China's AI program as a major threat, and want to restrain its growth by cutting off the supply of advanced chips to China. The resignation of Huang, who pressed the Trump Administration to allow the exports, seemed to signal that the hawks had found new influence in Washington.

By this weekend, Bessent had softened his public resistance to Huang's reinstatement. That came just in time for Nvidia's board to reappoint Huang as CEO at President Donald Trump's urging.

Whether any real damage was done to national security isn't clear. But it is safe to say that the incoming Democratic majority in Congress won't take Trump at his word when he said on CNBC Monday that the Department of Justice "did a pretty good investigation" that exonerated Huang.

Congress will look deeply into the worries Bessent himself raised about Huang's acknowledged romantic relationship with a woman The New York Times and others have reported was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia's board said it had received assurances from top U.S. officials and had concluded his return wasn't a national security risk.

A top concern is whether China was able to use the affair to help divert Nvidia's high-end artificial intelligence chips toward helping its military. Bessent in a Nov. 13 interview with CNBC outlined the stakes.

"Any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts the — it hurts our great companies and in the AI race it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Bessent said that his department and others had wanted to blacklist Megaspeed, the company previously run by Alice Huang, Jensen Huang's paramour. But "Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The Commerce Department is headed by Secretary Howard Lutnick, Bessent's rival.

Putting Huang back at Nvidia wasn't originally Bessent's preference. He said in a Fox interview on Nov. 15 that the board should "go outside the company" to find a new leader. But by this past weekend, Bessent had apparently gotten on board with Trump's wishes.

"Jensen is on the outside now," Bessent told CBS on Sunday, in trying to explain his apparent policy shift.

Commerce has since taken steps to tighten some restrictions meant to prevent U.S. chips from helping China advance in the AI race.

But there is bipartisan worry about the Nvidia episode. "I think we need to catalog the damage that's been done," Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., told The Wall Street Journal. He said Lutnick had not responded to letters he sent saying the administration should be "taking the threats of communist China more seriously."

But Trump has clearly put an upper limit on how aggressively anyone in his administration can push back on China. The president said Monday that he was swayed by the pageantry in his visit to China last week.

Xi "met me at the plane, which is something they've never done, which was a sign," Trump said.

Trump said he felt like he needed to intervene to back Huang because the stock market was plummeting in the wake of Nvidia's troubles — a development he also blamed on Democrats' midterm victory.

"I watched our country lose $5 trillion because of a bad election," he said. "If I didn't get involved, you'd probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come." Now, its stock performance will be "unbelievable."

Bessent on Sunday played down the market risks. The company "continues to be fantastically profitable, and there is near unlimited demand for their chips worldwide," he told CBS.

Lutnick has embraced Trump's transactional urges. He has backed deals in which the U.S. takes equity stakes in companies such as Intel, saying the taxpayer deserves some of the upside.

Lutnick in January said he had heard Huang pitch Trump on allowing Nvidia to sell its relatively more advanced H200 chips into China.

"There are strong — two sides. We shouldn't sell them any or we should sell them." Where does he stand? "Right in the middle," Lutnick told Bloomberg. Trump decided to go ahead with the deal.

Bessent has said he is "neither a hawk nor a dove" on China, and is simply trying to manage the ultra-sensitive relationship between the world's two biggest economies. But the upshot of the Huang affair seems to be that Bessent wasn't able to sway the president on a matter that seemed cut-and-dried just days ago.

Trump said he discussed whether Huang should return to Nvidia with Lutnick, Bessent and Vice President JD Vance. "Howard Lutnick was very good. Very, he's very professional," Trump said on CNBC Monday.

"I call them the opposites, Scott and Howard," Trump added, before declaring them "both good."
